The **Anglophone Crisis** ([French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language): *Crise anglophone*), also known as the **Ambazonia War**,[^cite_note-11] is an [ongoing armed conflict](2346678.md) in the [English-speaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) [Northwest](646814.md) and [Southwest](3028638.md) regions of [Cameroon](5447.md), between the Cameroonian government and [Ambazonian](841514.md) separatist groups, part of the long-standing [Anglophone problem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglophone_problem).[^cite_note-Africa-12] Following the suppression of [2016–17 protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016%E2%80%9317_Cameroonian_protests) by Cameroonian authorities, separatists in the Anglophone regions (formerly collectively known as the [Southern Cameroons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Cameroons)) launched a [guerrilla](12720.md) campaign and later proclaimed independence. Within two months, the government sent its army into the Anglophone regions.[^cite_note-journal-13]

Starting as a low-scale insurgency, the conflict spread to most parts of the Anglophone regions within a year.[^cite_note-acleddatapickingafight-14] By the summer of 2019, the government controlled the major cities and parts of the countryside, while the Ambazonian nationalists held parts of the countryside and regularly appeared in the major cities.[^cite_note-crisisgroupmay22019-6] Separatists have occasionally carried out raids into the neighboring [Francophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language) regions of [Littoral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Littoral_Region_\(Cameroon\)) and [West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Region_\(Cameroon\)).[^cite_note-15] Thousands of people have been killed in the war, and more than half a million have been forced to flee their homes.[^cite_note-crisisgroupmay22019-6] The Cameroonian government was supported by the Buhari administration in Nigeria,[^cite_note-16] while at least one Ambazonian group is allied to [Biafran](44431.md) separatists.[^cite_note-17]

Talks mediated by Switzerland in 2019 ultimately failed, and the [Ambazonian leadership crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambazonian_leadership_crisis) has complicated any diplomatic process.[^cite_note-reutersjul42020-18] Separatist leaders who were extradited from Nigeria in 2018 were in 2019 handed life sentences by a military tribunal. Facing mounting international pressure for a global ceasefire, in July 2020, Cameroon began negotiating with these imprisoned leaders.[^cite_note-theafricareportjul62020-19] The talks were held between [Sisiku Julius Ayuk Tabe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sisiku_Julius_Ayuk_Tabe) and other imprisoned leaders and representatives of the Cameroonian government. The talks outlined a series of conditions for the Cameroonian government to accept, which Ayuk Tabe said would create an "enabling environment" for substantial negotiations to occur.[^cite_note-thenewhumanitarian.org-20] These talks ultimately failed, and fighting continued.[^cite_note-21]

## Background

Main article: [Origins of the Anglophone Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origins_of_the_Anglophone_Crisis)



Monument raised on the 150th anniversary of the establishment of Victoria at Ambas Bay, from which the name "Ambazonia" derives.

The name "Ambazonia" is taken from Ambas Bay and *Ambozes*, the local name of the mouth of the [Wouri River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wouri_River).[^cite_note-Vine2004-22] This is where the English language was permanently established for the first time in Southern Cameroons, when missionary [Alfred Saker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Saker) founded a settlement of freed slaves by [Ambas Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambas_Bay) in 1858, which was later renamed Victoria (present-day [Limbe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limbe,_Cameroon)).[^cite_note-23] In 1884, the area became the British Ambas Bay Protectorate, with Victoria as its capital. Britain ceded the area to the [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire) territory of [Kamerun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamerun) in 1887. Germany had some trouble establishing control over the hinterlands of Victoria, and fought the [Bafut Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bafut_Wars) against local [fondoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fon_\(title\)) until 1907.[^cite_note-24]

Following [World War I](4764461.md) and the [Treaty of Versailles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Versailles), Kamerun was divided between a [French](5843419.md) and a British [League of Nations Mandate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations_Mandate). The French mandate was known as Cameroun, and comprised most of the former German territory. The British mandate was an elongated strip of land along the border of [Colonial Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Nigeria), consisting of Northern Cameroons and Southern Cameroons, including the historical Ambas Bay Protectorate. This territory was organized as [British Cameroons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Cameroons).[^cite_note-25]



Boundary changes of Cameroon, 1901–1972.

The British administered the territories through [indirect rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indirect_rule), allowing native authorities to administer the population according to their own traditions. In 1953, the Southern Cameroons delegation at a conference in London asked for a separate region. The British agreed, and Southern Cameroons became an autonomous region with its capital still at Buea. Elections were held in 1954 and the parliament met on 1 October 1954, with [E. M. L. Endeley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E._M._L._Endeley) as Premier.[^cite_note-26]

The United Nations organised a [plebiscite in the region on 11 February 1961](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1961_British_Cameroons_referendum) which put two alternatives to the people: union with Nigeria or union with Cameroon. The third option, independence, was opposed by the British representative to the UN Trusteeship Council, [Sir Andrew Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Cohen_\(statesman\)), and as a result was not listed. In the plebiscite, 60% of voters in the Northern Cameroons voted for union with Nigeria, while 70% of voters in the Southern Cameroons opted for union with Cameroon.[^cite_note-27] The results owed partly to a fear of domination by much larger Nigeria.[^cite_note-28] Endeley was defeated in elections on 1 February 1959 by [John Ngu Foncha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Ngu_Foncha).[^cite_note-29]

Southern Cameroons federated with Cameroon on 1 October 1961 as "[West Cameroon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Cameroon)", with its own [prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Prime_Ministers_of_Cameroon). However, the English-speaking peoples of the Southern Cameroons did not believe that they were fairly treated by the 80% majority French-speaking government of the country. Then-president [Ahmadou Ahidjo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadou_Ahidjo) feared that Southern Cameroons would secede from the union, taking its natural resources with it. Following a [French Cameroon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Cameroon) [unilateral referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameroonian_constitutional_referendum,_1972) on 20 May 1972, a new constitution was adopted in Cameroon which replaced the [federal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_republic) state with a [unitary state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state), and also gave more power to the president.[^cite_note-30] Southern Cameroons lost its autonomous status and became the [Northwest Region](646814.md) and the [Southwest Region](3028638.md) of the Republic of Cameroon. Pro-independence groups claimed that this violated the constitution, as the majority of deputies from West Cameroon had not consented to legitimize the constitutional changes.[^cite_note-31] They argued that Southern Cameroons had effectively been [annexed](18950961.md) by Cameroon.[^cite_note-32] Shortly afterwards, French Cameroun's political leadership changed the constitution again, appointed French-speaking Paul Biya as Prime Minister and successor to Ahmadou Ahidjo.

In a memorandum dated 20 March 1985, Anglophone lawyer and President of the Cameroon Bar Association [Fongum Gorji Dinka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fongum_Gorji_Dinka) wrote that the Cameroonian government led by [Paul Biya](392319.md) was unconstitutional and announced the former Southern Cameroons should become independent as the Republic of Ambazonia. Dinka was incarcerated the following January without trial.[^cite_note-33] Three years later, he escaped to Nigeria.[^cite_note-34]

In 1993, representatives of Anglophone groups convened the first All Anglophone Conference (AAC1) in Buea. The conference issued the "Buea Declaration", which called for constitutional amendments to restore the 1961 federation. This was followed by the second All Anglophone Conference (AAC2) in [Bamenda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamenda) in 1994. This conference issued the "Bamenda Declaration", which stated that if the federal state was not restored within a reasonable time, Southern Cameroons would declare its independence. The AAC was renamed the Southern Cameroons Peoples Conference (SCPC), and later the Southern Cameroons Peoples Organisation (SCAPO), with the [Southern Cameroons National Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Cameroons_National_Council) (SCNC) as the executive governing body. Younger activists formed the [Southern Cameroons Youth League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Cameroons_Youth_League) (SCYL) in Buea on 28 May 1995. The SCNC sent a delegation, led by John Foncha, to the United Nations, which was received on 1 June 1995 and presented a petition against the 'annexation' of the Southern Cameroons by French Cameroon. This was followed by a signature referendum the same year, which the organisers claim produced a 99% vote in favour of independence with 315,000 people voting.[^cite_note-35]

SCNC activities were routinely disrupted by police.[^cite_note-Murison-36] On 23 March 1997, about ten people were killed in a raid on a gendarme camp in [Bamenda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamenda). The police arrested between 200 and 300 people, mostly SCNC supporters, but also members of the [Social Democratic Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Front_\(Cameroon\)), an opposition party with significant support in the Anglophone regions.[^cite_note-ecoi-37] In the subsequent trials, [Amnesty International](18947898.md) and the SCNC found substantive evidence of admissions through torture and force.[^cite_note-Murison-36] The raid and trial resulted in a shutdown of SCNC activities.[^cite_note-CQ-38] In response to this, in April 1998 a small faction elected Esoka Ndoki Mukete, a high-ranking member of the Social Democratic Front, as the new chair of the SCNC. In October 1999, when many of the accused were found guilty in the 1997 trial, the faction led by Mukete became more assertive. On 1 October 1999, militants took over Radio Buea to proclaim the independence of Southern Cameroons, but failed to do so before security forces intervened.[^cite_note-39] The leadership and many members of the SCNC were subsequently arrested.[^cite_note-CQ-38] After clashes with the police, the SCNC was officially declared illegal by the Cameroonian authorities in 2001.[^cite_note-40] In 2006, a faction of SCNC once again declared the independence of Ambazonia.[^cite_note-CQ-38]

## Prelude

Main article: [2016–17 Cameroonian protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016%E2%80%9317_Cameroonian_protests)

On 6 October 2016, the [Cameroon Anglophone Civil Society Consortium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameroon_Anglophone_Civil_Society_Consortium), an organization consisting of lawyer and teacher trade unions in the Anglophone regions, initiated a strike.[^cite_note-41] Led by Barrister [Agbor Balla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agbor_Balla), [Fontem Neba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fontem_Neba), and Tassang Wilfred, they were protesting against the appointment of [Francophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language) judges in the Anglophone regions.[^cite_note-42] They saw this as threatening the [common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law) system in the Anglophone regions,[^cite_note-43] as well as part of the general marginalization of Anglophones.[^cite_note-44] The strikes were supported by peaceful protests in the cities of [Bamenda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamenda), [Buea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buea) and [Limbe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limbe,_Cameroon). The activists demanded protection of the law system of the Anglophone regions and opposed the civil law system used by the Francophone magistrate replacing the common law system. They asked for several laws to be translated into English, and that the common law system should be taught at the [University of Buea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Buea) and the [University of Bamenda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Bamenda).[^cite_note-45]

The Cameroonian government deployed security forces to crack down the protests. Protesters were attacked with tear gas, and protesters and lawyers were allegedly assaulted by soldiers.[^cite_note-46] Throughout November 2016, thousands of teachers in the Anglophone regions joined the lawyers' strike. All schools in the Anglophone regions were shut down.[^cite_note-47]

Two weeks into the protests, more than 100 protesters had been arrested, and six were reported dead. Unconfirmed videos released over social media showed various violent scenes, including the beating of protesters by policemen.[^cite_note-48]

In January 2017, the Cameroonian government set up a committee to initiate a dialogue with the lawyer activists. The lawyers refused to talk, demanding that all arrested activists be released before any dialogue. The lawyers submitted a draft for a federal state, and the government responded by banning their movements altogether. The protesters were now openly regarded as a security threat by the Cameroonian government, and more arrests followed.[^cite_note-49] The government also implemented an Internet blockade in cities across the Anglophone regions.[^cite_note-\:11-50]

At this point, the crisis began to attract international responses. More than 13,000 Anglophone Cameroonians living in Maryland protested against the Cameroonian government crackdown. On 27 June, United States Congressman [Anthony Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_Brown_\(Maryland_politician\)) filed a petition with the [United States Secretary of State](32293.md), [Rex Tillerson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rex_Tillerson), to call for the government of Cameroon to immediately show concern and solve the ongoing crises.[^cite_note-51] The [United States](3434750.md) condemned the loss of life and brutality against Anglophone protesters.[^cite_note-52] Many separatists and federalists hoped that the crackdown would lead to an international military intervention against the Cameroonian government.[^cite_note-53]

The government crackdown on the protests contributed to mainstream separatist movements. In September 2017, Ambazonian separatists began to take up arms against the government.[^cite_note-54]

## Timeline

Main article: [Timeline of the Anglophone Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Anglophone_Crisis)

### 2017

Main article: [Timeline of the Anglophone Crisis (2017)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Anglophone_Crisis_\(2017\))

In early September 2017, the [Ambazonia Governing Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambazonia_Governing_Council) (AGovC), a separatist movement established in 2013 through the merger of several groups,[^cite_note-crisisgroupmay22019-6] formally deployed the [Ambazonia Defence Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambazonia_Defence_Forces) (ADF) in Southern Cameroons. On 9 September, the ADF carried out its first military action, attacking a military base in Besongabang, [Manyu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manyu_\(department\)). Three Cameroonian soldiers were killed in the attack, while the ADF claimed their soldiers managed to return to base unreduced.[^cite_note-Sept_13-1] Throughout September, separatists carried out two bombings; one targeting security forces in [Bamenda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamenda),[^cite_note-55] and another targeting police officers. While the first bombing failed, the second injured three policemen.[^cite_note-56] On 22 September, Cameroonian soldiers opened fire on protesters, killing at least five and injuring many more.[^cite_note-22Sep-57]



Streets of Buea on a ghost town day, 30 September.

On 1 October, the [Southern Cameroons Ambazonia Consortium United Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Cameroons_Ambazonia_Consortium_United_Front) (SCACUF), an umbrella movement consisting of several independence movements, unilaterally declared the independence of Ambazonia. This declaration was followed by mass demonstrations across the Anglophone regions. The separatists strategically chose this date, as it is the anniversary for the unification of Cameroon and Southern Cameroons. SCACUF would later transform itself into the [Interim Government of Ambazonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interim_Government_of_Ambazonia) (IG). Several months into the war, the IG would reject the idea of an armed struggle, preferring instead a diplomatic campaign and [civil disobedience](37073.md). This stance would eventually change.[^cite_note-58]

The Cameroonian Army moved into the regions in force to fight the separatists and quell the demonstrations. Throughout the day, at least eight demonstrators were killed in [Buea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buea) and Bamenda.[^cite_note-2_Oct-59] The Cameroonian military also reinforced the Nigerian border, and on 9 October, it claimed to have stopped "hundreds of Nigerian fighters" from crossing into Cameroon.[^cite_note-60]

Throughout November, eight soldiers, at least 14 civilians and five fugitives were confirmed killed due to the conflict.[^cite_note-Nov_29-61] Separatists killed two [gendarmes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gendarmerie) in Bamenda in the first week of the month.[^cite_note-62] On the last two days of November, five soldiers and five policemen were killed in two separate separatist attacks near [Mamfe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamfe).[^cite_note-voanov302017-63] On 1 December, the Cameroonian government ordered the evacuation of 16 villages in Manyu ahead of a military offensive,[^cite_note-64] and on 4 December it formally declared war on the separatists.[^cite_note-Dec_4-65] The Cameroonian Army moved into Manyu, retaking two villages on 7 December[^cite_note-66] and securing Mamfe by 15 December, partly with elite troops.[^cite_note-67] During the offensive, the ADF carried out guerrilla attacks on the Cameroonian Army,[^cite_note-68] killing at least seven soldiers throughout December.[^cite_note-jan62018-69] On 18 December, the Cameroonian Army began to destroy dozens of civilian homes in retaliation, and killed several civilians.[^cite_note-jan62018-69] The December offensive also saw occasional spillover across the Nigerian border.[^cite_note-Dec_20-70]

By the end of 2017, several separatist militias had emerged, with the ADF standing out as the most prominent one.[^cite_note-forces-71] During the guerrilla campaign in [Manyu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manyu_\(department\)) and [Mezam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mezam), it had clashed with the army 13 times.[^cite_note-BBCoct42018-72] The separatists had also spread out, and by the end of the year, they were active in five [departments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Departments_of_Cameroon).[^cite_note-acleddatapickingafight-14]

### 2018

Main article: [Timeline of the Anglophone Crisis (2018)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Anglophone_Crisis_\(2018\))

The separatists suffered a major setback at the beginning of 2018. On 5 January, members of the IG were detained by Nigerian authorities,[^cite_note-73] which proceeded to hand them over to Cameroon. A total of 69 leaders and activists were [extradited](239648.md) to Cameroon and subsequently arrested, including [President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Ambazonia) [Sisiku Julius Ayuk Tabe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sisiku_Julius_Ayuk_Tabe). Since most of the individuals had submitted asylum claims,[^cite_note-leadership_UNPO-74] the deportation immediately became controversial, as it possibly violated the Nigerian constitution.[^cite_note-journalducameroun.com-75] They spent 10 months at a [gendarme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gendarmerie) headquarters, before being transferred to a maximum security prison in [Yaoundé](57671.md).[^cite_note-journalducameroun.com-75] [Samuel Ikome Sako](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Ikome_Sako) was announced as Acting President on 4 February, replacing Tabe for the time being.[^cite_note-Sako-76]

January saw an escalation in guerrilla attacks on symbolic targets,[^cite_note-77] as well as repeated spillover into Nigeria.[^cite_note-78] The separatists began to target traditional village chiefs, as well as local administrators whom they accused of siding with the security forces.[^cite_note-79] Attacks on gendarmes intensified,[^cite_note-thursfeb1-80] and gunmen also began to target schools to enforce a school boycott.[^cite_note-81] On 24 February, separatists abducted the government's regional representative for social affairs in the [Northwest Region](646814.md), apparently looking to exchange him for imprisoned separatist activists.[^cite_note-82]

The [Battle of Batibo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Batibo) was fought on 3 March,[^cite_note-83] producing unprecedented casualties on both sides[^cite_note-mar202018-84] and forcing over 4,000 locals to flee.[^cite_note-85] On 20 March, Cameroonian soldiers freed two Cameroonian and one Tunisian hostage in [Meme Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meme_\(department\)).[^cite_note-mar202018-84] A similar operation freed seven Swiss, six Cameroonian and five Italian hostages on 4 April, prompting the [ADF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambazonia_Defence_Forces) to declare that it did not take hostages nor target foreigners.[^cite_note-86] On 25 April, separatists forced the Cameroonian Army to retreat from the town of [Belo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belo,_Cameroon).[^cite_note-87]

Throughout May, there were heavy clashes in [Mbonge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mbonge)[^cite_note-2May-88] and [Muyuka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muyuka), resulting in heavy casualties.[^cite_note-89] On 20 May, in an effort to boycott celebrations of Cameroon's [National Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Day_\(Cameroon\)), Ambazonian forces struck in several villages throughout Southern Cameroons, including [Konye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konye,_Cameroon), [Batibo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batibo) and Ekona,[^cite_note-May20-90] and abducted the mayor of the town of [Bangem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangem).[^cite_note-91] On 24 May, Cameroonian soldiers killed at least 30 people while storming a hotel in Pinyin.[^cite_note-92] By the end of May, Cameroon had also retaken Belo, though fighting continued around the town, which was almost completely abandoned by its inhabitants.[^cite_note-thisisagenocide-93]

In mid-June, Ambazonian forces started a blockade of the [Kumba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumba,_Cameroon)-[Buea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buea) highway at Ekona, a town located approximately 10 kilometers from Buea. A military assault on the separatists in Ekona failed to lift the blockade.[^cite_note-94] While casualties related to the battle of Ekona remain unconfirmed, the Cameroonian government later declared that more than 40 soldiers and policemen died in the later half of June across Southern Cameroons.[^cite_note-100deadvoa-95] By now, the war had fully extended to Buea, with separatists mounting road blocks and attacking government soldiers on 29 June.[^cite_note-96] Attacks in Buea intensified in July, with one invasion on 1 July,[^cite_note-97] another on 9 July[^cite_note-98] and another on 30 July.[^cite_note-99]



Scorched vehicles left behind after clashes in Buea on 11 September.

On 16 August, separatists attacked a [convoy](42990.md) transporting a member of parliament in [Babungo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babungo_\(village\)), [Ngo-Ketunjia Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngo-Ketunjia), killing at least four soldiers. A civilian who got caught in the crossfire was also killed.[^cite_note-16.08.2018-100] Eight days later, a successful guerrilla attack killed two gendarmes and wounded a brigade commander in Zhoa in [Wum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wum), [Northwest Region](646814.md).[^cite_note-101] As a response, the Cameroonian Army burned down the village.[^cite_note-102]

September saw some setbacks for the separatists, as the government launched attacks into the rural areas they controlled.[^cite_note-crisisgroupmay22019-6] There was heavy fighting in [Muyuka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muyuka), where Cameroon launched an offensive.[^cite_note-103] The Cameroonian Army enjoyed some success in weeding out separatist camps. In a particularly lethal raid on 4 September, Cameroonian soldiers killed 27 suspected separatists near Zhoa.[^cite_note-104] Another raid on separatist camps near [Chomba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chomba) killed at least 15 separatists.[^cite_note-105] The separatists also had their successes; On 9 September 50 or more separatists successfully carried out three coordinated attacks on multiple targets in Oku, burning down the police station, destroying the Assistant Division Officer's belongings, stealing a police van and abducting three police officers.[^cite_note-106] On 11 September, separatists took control of two neighborhoods of Buea, blocking the main entrances to the city and killing a soldier from the [Rapid Intervention Brigade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_Intervention_Brigade) (BIR).[^cite_note-107] An attempt was also made to abduct the Fon of Buea.[^cite_note-108] On 27 September, separatists forced the police and gendarmes to retreat from [Balikumbat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balikumbat), [Ngo-Ketunjia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngo-Ketunjia).[^cite_note-109] Despite government offensives in September, the separatists remained in control of many rural and urban areas.[^cite_note-crisisgroupmay22019-6]

On 30 September, in anticipation of the first anniversary of Ambazonia's declaration of independence on 1 October, the authorities imposed a 48-hour curfew throughout the Anglophone region. This was done to prevent a re-occurrence of the mass demonstrations that took place the year before. People were forbidden from moving across sub-division boundaries, and gatherings of four or more people in public were prohibited. Businesses were shut down and motor parks were closed as well. Meanwhile, in anticipation of the [Cameroonian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameroonian_presidential_election,_2018) on 7 October, the separatists started enforcing a lock-down of their own, blocking major highways with trees or car wrecks. Throughout the day, security forces and separatists clashed in Buea, Bamenda and other cities.[^cite_note-110]

By October, the conflict had spread to most of Southern Cameroons. The ADF alone had clashed with the army 83 times,[^cite_note-BBCoct42018-72] separatist militias were now active in 12 divisions, and attacks were now more lethal.[^cite_note-acleddatapickingafight-14] According to an [International Crisis Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Crisis_Group) analyst, by October the war had reached a stalemate, with the army being unable to defeat the separatists, while the separatists were not militarily strong enough to expel the army.[^cite_note-BBCoct42018-72] Others described the separatists as severely outgunned and on the verge of defeat, mostly due to their lack of access to proper weapons.[^cite_note-viceoct2018-111] On 7 October, the day of the [Cameroonian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameroonian_presidential_election,_2018), there were clashes all over Southern Cameroons with both sides blaming the other; the Cameroonian government claimed that separatists moved to prevent what they considered a foreign election to take place in the Anglophone regions,[^cite_note-112] while the separatists blamed the government for instigating the violence.[^cite_note-113] This resulted in a very low turnout, as "more than 90 percent of residents" fled violence in some localities; and in many cases no officials showed up to man the polling stations.[^cite_note-\:0-114] In Bamenda, at least 20 separatist fighters moved around openly to prevent people from voting. Two separatists were killed by government troops while attacking a polling station.[^cite_note-115] Following the election, two people from Kumba were murdered for having voted.[^cite_note-116] On 17 October, [SDF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Front_\(Cameroon\)) President [John Fru Ndi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Fru_Ndi)'s house in Bamenda was set on fire by armed men.[^cite_note-117] On 23 October, the Cameroonian Army launched simultaneous attacks on seven or more separatist camps in the Northwest Region, initiating battles that continued for more than 24 hours. At least 30 separatists were killed, as well as an unknown number of Cameroonian soldiers.[^cite_note-118]

In the beginning of November, 79 students and four staff members [were kidnapped from a school in Nkwen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PSS_Nkwen_kidnapping), near Bamenda.[^cite_note-119] All 79 students were released without ransom three days later.[^cite_note-120] The Ambazonia Self-Defence Council claimed that they not only had nothing to do with the kidnappings, but had also sent its own fighters to try to locate the children.[^cite_note-121] November also saw several major confrontations. On 11 November, according to the separatists, 13 Cameroonian soldiers and two separatists were killed when separatists carried out a successful ambush.[^cite_note-122] The next day, Cameroonian soldiers ambushed and killed at least 13 suspected separatists in [Donga-Mantung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donga-Mantung),[^cite_note-123] and another 25 near [Nkambé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nkamb%C3%A9) the next day.[^cite_note-124] On 22 November, around 40 Ambazonian fighters and unarmed civilians were killed in [Bali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bali,_Cameroon) by government soldiers, who then set their corpses on fire. With no trace of bullet wounds on any of the bodies, unconfirmed reports alleged the use of chemicals by the soldiers.[^cite_note-125] On 28 November, separatists blocked the Buea-Kumba Highway.[^cite_note-jdcdec3-126] The month also saw the first major spillover into other parts of Cameroon; On 29 November, at least 30 people were kidnapped by ten unidentified gunmen in [Bangourain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangourain), [West Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Region_\(Cameroon\)), and transported with canoes across the [Lake Bamendjing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noun_River_\(Cameroon\)) reservoir.[^cite_note-127] A month later, two suspected separatists were lynched by the villagers, and the Cameroonian Army launched an offensive nearby.[^cite_note-128] Bangourain was attacked once more on 22 December, prompting the separatists to accuse the government of carrying out a [false flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_flag) operation to incite Cameroonian Francophones.[^cite_note-cnadec2618-129] In Kembong, just south of [Mamfe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamfe), a [military vehicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_vehicle) hit a road bomb; no soldiers died, but the vehicle was destroyed.[^cite_note-130]



Cameroonian soldiers on a bridge, heading to [Wum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wum), December 2018.

December saw more burning of houses by government soldiers in Romajia,[^cite_note-cnadec72018-131] Kikiakom and Ekona.[^cite_note-cnadec72018-131] On 15 December, at least five separatists were summarily executed, possibly by fellow separatists.[^cite_note-132] On 21 December, ADF General [Ivo Mbah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivo_Mbah) was killed during a military raid in Kumba.[^cite_note-133]

On 31 December, the presidents of Cameroon and Ambazonia addressed the ongoing conflict in their end of year speeches. President [Paul Biya](392319.md) of Cameroon promised to "neutralize" all separatists who refused to disarm, while emphasizing that anyone who drops their guns can be reintegrated into society.[^cite_note-134] Interim President Sako of Ambazonia said that the separatists would switch from a defensive to an offensive strategy in the war, and announced that a Mobile Wing Police would be created to capture territory and defeat government militias. He also condemned anyone engaged in kidnappings of civilians, and promised to fight back against anyone involved in such practices.[^cite_note-135] The same night, separatist fighters attacked the convoy of the Governor of the [Northwest Region](646814.md), injuring at least one government soldier.[^cite_note-136] The Cameroonian Army also announced the killing of [Lekeaka Oliver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lekeaka_Oliver), Field Marshall of the [Red Dragon militia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Dragon_\(Ambazonian_militia\)), in [Lebialem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebialem); the killing was denied by the IG,[^cite_note-Oliver-137] and was also denied by sources within the Cameroonian Army. Oliver resurfaced in a video a week later, proving that reports of his death were false.[^cite_note-jdcjan42019-138]

### 2019

Main article: [Timeline of the Anglophone Crisis (2019)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Anglophone_Crisis_\(2019\))

2019 started off with further escalation of the war. On 6 January, Anglophone Cameroonians in the diaspora organized protests to mark the first anniversary of the arrest of the Ambazonian leadership. Throughout the day, armed clashes took place in [Muyuka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muyuka), [Bafut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bafut,_Cameroon), Mundum and [Mamfe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamfe).[^cite_note-139] In Mamfe, two Ambazonian generals were killed when their camps were raided by the BIR.[^cite_note-140] On 24 January, General Andrew Ngoe of [SOCADEF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SOCADEF) was killed in Matoh, [Mbonge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mbonge).[^cite_note-AndrewNgoe-141]

January also saw the start of the trials of the Ambazonian leaders, including Ayuk Tabe, who had spent a year in prison. The trial was complicated by all the Ambazonian leaders rejecting their Cameroonian nationality, which the court ultimately ignored. The leaders then refused to be tried by Francophone judges. On 1 March, the trial took a diplomatic twist; a Nigerian court determined that the deportation of the Ambazonian leadership had been unconstitutional, and ordered their return to Nigeria. Nevertheless, the trial resumed.[^cite_note-mar12019abuja-142]

The separatists got bolder with enforcing lockdowns. The same day as the start of the trial, the streets of Buea were almost completely deserted.[^cite_note-143] On 4 February, in anticipation of [Youth Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Cameroon) on 11 February, separatists declared a 10-day lockdown, telling people all across the Anglophone regions to stay at home from 5–14 February.[^cite_note-144] The lockdown was a matter of controversy among the separatists, with a spokesperson of the [Ambazonia Defence Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambazonia_Defence_Forces) arguing that it would be counterproductive.[^cite_note-145] The next day, large parts of Buea were closed down, while armed clashes took place in Muea and Muyuka. Most of Bamenda was also closed down, with smaller clashes taking place.[^cite_note-feb52019-146] In Muyuka, an Ambazonian Colonel was killed alongside two other separatist fighters.[^cite_note-147] In Mbengwi, two separatists died while attacking the Divisional Office there.[^cite_note-148] As a result of the lockdown, Youth Day celebrations had negligible turnout in Southern Cameroons. In Bamenda, the Governor of Northwest Region, Adolph Lele l'Afrique, was escorted by soldiers to attend a small celebration. The military escort came under fire while driving to the ceremony, possibly resulting in casualties. The celebrations were boycotted in most major cities in the Anglophone regions, including Buea, Kumbo, Belo, Ndop, Ndu, Wum, Muyuka, Mamfe, Tombem, Mundemba and Lebialem, while there was a comparatively significant turnout in Nkambe.[^cite_note-149]

Early March saw two serious kidnapping incidents; one on the Kumba-Buea Highway, which was quickly foiled by the BIR,[^cite_note-150] and one at the University of Buea, where more than 15 students were kidnapped, beaten and released.[^cite_note-151] The separatists set up several road blocks, including an overnight mission where the [Seven Karta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Karta) militia blocked the Bafut-Bamenda Highway with concrete walls[^cite_note-152] On 14 March, Cameroonian soldiers burned down several houses in Dunga Mantung and Menchum, and killed at least 12 people (several of whom were burned alive).[^cite_note-153] At the end of March, an ADF leader announced that they would take the war into the French-speaking parts of Cameroon. A week later, separatists – possibly the ADF – raided Penda Mboko, [Littoral Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Littoral_Region_\(Cameroon\)), and injured three gendarmes.[^cite_note-jdc2apr2019-154] This was done in defiance of the IG.[^cite_note-bareta25dec2018-155]

On 31 March, various Ambazonian independence movements agreed to form the [Southern Cameroons Liberation Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Cameroons_Liberation_Council) (SCLC), a united front consisting of both separatists and federalists.[^cite_note-156] On 4 April, separatists imposed a 10-day lockdown on Buea.[^cite_note-157] On 9 April, the newly established SCLC declared an early end to lockdown, citing how it mainly affected civilians. However, the [Ambazonia Self-Defence Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambazonia_Self-Defence_Council) (ASC), the armed wing of the IG, declared that the SCLC had no authority to call off the lockdown without consulting it first.[^cite_note-158]



Cameroonian soldiers in Bamenda in May 2019.

On 14 April, four Cameroonian soldiers and three separatist fighters were killed in [Bali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bali,_Cameroon).[^cite_note-159] In Ekona, at least six people were killed in a government offensive.[^cite_note-160] The next day, security forces carried out mass arrests in Buea as they searched for separatist bases.[^cite_note-161] On 24 April, gunmen burned down the house of the mayor of [Fundong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fundong).[^cite_note-162] On 27 April, [SDF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Front_\(Cameroon\)) leader [John Fru Ndi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Fru_Ndi) [was kidnapped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kidnapping_of_John_Fru_Ndi) in Kumbo by separatists, but was released the same day. Fru Ndi had travelled to Kumbo to attend the funeral of Joseph Banadzem, the Parliamentary group leader of the SDF. Local separatist fighters had consented to the funeral taking place, provided that no Francophone Cameroonians attended it.[^cite_note-163] On 30 April, government soldiers killed one person and burned at least 10 houses in Kikaikelaiki, [Bui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bui_\(Cameroon_department\)).[^cite_note-164]

May saw the Anglophone Crisis get some international attention. On 5 May, following appeals by the [European Union Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union_Parliament), it was announced that the Anglophone Crisis would be debated at the [United Nations Security Council](31956.md).[^cite_note-165] Days later, the Cameroonian government announced it was ready for an "inclusive" dialogue where anything except Ambazonian independence could be discussed.[^cite_note-166] Meanwhile, a [leadership crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Ambazonian_leadership_crisis) erupted within the IG, forcing smaller separatist movements to pick their loyalties.[^cite_note-167]

In June, the separatists announced that they had started producing their own weapons in the warzone. Shortly afterwards, a mine killed four policemen and wounded another six,[^cite_note-producingownweapons-168] convincing many that the separatists were no longer outgunned, but indeed well-armed.[^cite_note-voajun192019-169] The same month, it was revealed that the government of Cameroon and separatist movements would enter negotiations.[^cite_note-170] On 27 June, John Fru Ndi was [kidnapped for the second time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kidnapping_of_John_Fru_Ndi#Second_kidnapping) in two months.[^cite_note-171]

Heavy fighting took place in July. On 3 July, separatists ambushed a military boat carrying 13 Cameroonian soldiers on the Ekpambiri river, [Manyu Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manyu_\(department\)). Three soldiers managed to escape, two were rescued, one was found dead after five days, and seven remain unaccounted for. The Cameroonian Army declared that they knew which base the attackers had come from, and that an operation would be launched to destroy it.[^cite_note-172] On 8 July, at least two gendarmes were killed and several more were wounded in an ambush in [Ndop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ndop,_Cameroon).[^cite_note-173] On 11 July, at least seven separatist fighters were killed in Esu.[^cite_note-174] The next day, armed men kidnapped 30 bus passengers passing through [Belo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belo,_Cameroon), in an apparent case of infighting among local separatist militias.[^cite_note-175] One Cameroonian soldier and three separatists were killed in Buea on 14 July,[^cite_note-176] and at least one Cameroonian soldier and at least five separatists were killed in [Mbiame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mbiame) three days later.[^cite_note-177]



Police forces deploy at Kondengui Central Prison, [Yaounde](57671.md), during the [July 2019 Cameroon prison riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_2019_Cameroon_prison_riots).

Towards the end of July, [two major prison riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_2019_Cameroon_prison_riots) took place. On 22 July, separatist inmates at [Kondengui Central Prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kondengui_Central_Prison) took part in staging a protest against the prison conditions. The protest escalated into a riot, which was eventually quelled violently. A similar protest and crackdown took place at Buea Central Prison a few days later.[^cite_note-178] More than 100 inmates were moved to undisclosed locations for detention,[^cite_note-jul292019-179] some of whom were subjected to torture,[^cite_note-180] and there were speculations that some had been killed during the riots. The IG demanded that the Cameroonian government account for the missing inmates, threatening to impose a lockdown encompassing the entire Anglophone regions if it failed to do so.[^cite_note-jul262019-181] The lockdown started on 29 July.[^cite_note-182] Midnight to 31 July, the ten detained members of the IG, including Ayuk Tabe, went on a hunger strike over the missing convicts.[^cite_note-183]

On 4 August, separatist fighters ambushed and killed a soldier and his civilian driver in Penda Mboko, [Littoral Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Littoral_Region_\(Cameroon\)), the second attack there since March.[^cite_note-184] On 14 August, in an effort to sabotage the new school year, separatist militias announced that region-wide lockdowns would be enforced on 2–6 September and 9–13, covering the first two weeks of the academic year.[^cite_note-185] On 15 August, at least seven soldiers from the BIR were injured when their vehicle skidded off the road in Kumba; while separatists claimed responsibility for the incident, the army claimed it was simply a road accident.[^cite_note-186]

On 20 August, the ten detained members of the IG, including Ayuk Tabe, were sentenced to [life imprisonment](148681.md) by the Yaoundé Military Tribunal.[^cite_note-187] This was followed by a military escalation and a series of lockdowns, crushing hopes that the school year could start as normal in early September. Within a week, violence and uncertainty had triggered tens of thousands of people to flee from the Anglophone regions.[^cite_note-voaaug262019-188] Due to the escalation, a government-led effort to reopen at least 4,500 schools by 2 September ultimately failed.[^cite_note-189]

In September, President Paul Biya announced the [Major National Dialogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_National_Dialogue), a political dialogue that would be held before the end of the month.[^cite_note-Cameroon_2019._p_12-190] While separatists were quick to reject the initiative, citing the recent life sentences handed to the separatist leaders,[^cite_note-191] opposition parties in Cameroon showed interest in the initiative and started submitting proposals.[^cite_note-192] Meanwhile, the war continued; on 20 September, separatist fighters launched a failed attack on a hotel in Bamenda where several government officials were staying.[^cite_note-193] While the Major National Dialogue was happening, a significant number of Anglophones celebrated Ambazonia's third independence day on 1 October. Nine people were killed in clashes all across the Anglophone regions that day,[^cite_note-194] while "Field Marshall" Lekeaka Oliver of the [Red Dragon militia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Dragon_\(Ambazonian_militia\)) used the occasion to declare himself Paramount Ruler of [Lebialem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebialem).[^cite_note-195] October also saw the public surrender of Ambazonian general Ekeom Polycarp, who was subsequently assassinated by other separatists.[^cite_note-196] The separatists also started opening "community schools" across the Anglophone regions, providing an alternative to the government-run schools which they had boycotted for years.[^cite_note-community_schools-197]

On 1 December, separatist fighters carried out their first attack on an airplane, firing at a [Camair-Co](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camair-Co) passenger plane while it was landing at [Bamenda Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamenda_Airport). No one was wounded in the attack, although several bullets penetrated into the cabin. This incident was the first of its kind, and possibly indicated that the separatists would take on larger government targets.[^cite_note-198] The AGovC announced that the airline in question often transported soldiers, and that they thus considered their aircraft to be legitimate targets.[^cite_note-199] The separatists also started their campaign to sabotage the upcoming [2020 Cameroonian parliamentary election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Cameroonian_parliamentary_election), and within a week they abducted some 40 politicians, including two mayors and at least 19 councilors.[^cite_note-200] A separatist attack on a civilian truck with a military escort left some three civilians dead on 19 December.[^cite_note-201] Cameroon also launched a series of operations in Mezam, Boyo, Donga Mantung, Bui, Ngo Ketunjia and Boyo that would, over six days, force around 5,500 people to flee their homes.[^cite_note-202] In their respective New Year speeches on 31 December, President Paul Biya of Cameroon and Samuel Ikome Sako of Ambazonia both promised to intensify the war in 2020.[^cite_note-203]

### 2020

Main article: [Timeline of the Anglophone Crisis (2020)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Anglophone_Crisis_\(2020\))



Cameroonian gendarmes arrive in [Buea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buea), 9 January 2020.

Separatist forces started the new year by declaring a five-day lockdown for February, intended to prevent the upcoming parliamentary election from taking place in the Anglophone regions.[^cite_note-204] Clashes took place in Buea and Muyuka, where separatists burned down the office of the Divisional Officer.[^cite_note-205] On 6 January, after failing to listen to demands from fellow separatists not to terrorize civilians, separatist commander "General Divine" was assassinated by his own men.[^cite_note-206] Following instances of popular uprisings against the separatists in Balikumbat and Babungo, the ADF ordered its fighters to take action against anyone caught terrorizing civilians.[^cite_note-voajan82020-207] January also saw what was possibly the most serious instance of separatist infighting to date; the [Southern Cameroons Restoration Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Cameroons_Restoration_Forces) (loyal to the IG) abducted 40 fighters from the Ambazonia Defence Forces, six of whom were subsequently murdered.[^cite_note-voainfightingjan-208] "General Chacha", the separatist commander responsible for the abduction, was captured and summarily executed by Cameroonian soldiers a week later.[^cite_note-209]

A [parliamentary election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Cameroonian_parliamentary_election) was held in February, and was marked by unprecedented escalation on both sides. Hundreds of additional troops were deployed to combat separatist attempts to sabotage the election, while the separatists abducted 120 politicians within the two weeks preceding the election. Efforts by the Cameroonian government to prevent a repeat of the debacle surrounding the 2018 presidential election ultimately failed, as the turnout in the Anglophone regions was very low. The Ambazonians, who claimed that 98 percent of the population boycotted the election, hailed this as a great victory.[^cite_note-cnafeb92020-210] The results in 11 constituencies were later nullified due to separatist activities, triggering a partial re-election in March. The results were largely the same, with a marginal turnout and clashes taking place throughout the day.[^cite_note-211] Less than a week after the February election, Cameroonian soldiers and armed Fulani carried out the [Ngarbuh massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngarbuh_massacre), killing at least 23 civilians in Ntumbo, Northwest Region and drawing international condemnation.[^cite_note-212]

On 1 March, Cameroonian soldiers raped 20 women, killed one man, and burned and plundered houses in Ebam, Southwest Region. 36 people were taken to a military camp, where many were subjected to severe beating and torture. One person later died in captivity. The others were released between 4–6 March, after their families had paid money.[^cite_note-213]

On 7 March, around 20 separatist fighters carried out attacks on a police station and gendarmerie in [Galim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galim,_Cameroon), [West Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Region_\(Cameroon\)), killing two gendarmes, two police officers and four civilians. This was the third separatist attack in West Region.[^cite_note-trtmar92020-214] The next day, an [improvised explosive device](265112.md) killed one soldier and injured four soldiers, two police officers and one civilian in Bamenda.[^cite_note-215] Cameroon subsequently carried out an offensive in [Ngo-Ketunjia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngo-Ketunjia), where it claimed to have destroyed 10 separatist camps and killed at least 20 fighters, including some of those who had participated in the Galim raid.[^cite_note-216]

On 26 March, SOCADEF declared a two-week ceasefire to give people time to get tested for [COVID-19](63030231.md),[^cite_note-217] a move that was welcomed by the [United Nations](31769.md).[^cite_note-218] The IG and the AGovC declared their willingness to have their armed groups follow suit, but conditioned it on international monitoring and Cameroonian troops being confined to their bases.[^cite_note-219] The same day as SOCADEF's unilateral ceasefire went into effect and, coincidentally, the same day as the first COVID-19 case was confirmed in the Anglophone regions, President Sako declared the shutting of Ambazonia's borders and restriction of movement and public gatherings in order to limit the spreading of the virus, effective from 1 April.[^cite_note-220] However, the danger posed by COVID-19 did not bring about an immediate end of hostilities on the ground; just the day before Sako's declaration, separatists attacked an armored car, killing a soldier and 11 officials, including two deputy mayors.[^cite_note-221] On 8 April, responding to claims by the Cameroonian government that the war was nearing an end, separatists attacked military bases and destroyed infrastructure in villages all across the Anglophone regions.[^cite_note-voaapr92020-222] While SOCADEF extended its unilateral ceasefire,[^cite_note-223] separatists carried out coordinated attacks on several villages,[^cite_note-224] assassinated members of a traditional council[^cite_note-225] and abducted three government officials.[^cite_note-226] Towards the end of the month, Cameroon initiated [Operation Free Bafut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Free_Bafut), a series of raids in and around Bafut that aimed at completely rooting out separatist presence in the area. On 1 May, Cameroon announced the deaths of two separatist commanders known as General Alhaji and General Peace Plant; separatists confirmed the loss.[^cite_note-freebafutmay12020-227] There were also civilian casualties; one person was killed by soldiers on the first day of the operation.[^cite_note-228] Local villagers claimed that 13 civilians were killed in the fighting; this was denied by the Cameroonian Army.[^cite_note-voamay42020-229]



Cameroonian soldiers entering a town, April 2020.

On 10 May, separatist fighters assassinated the newly elected mayor of Mamfe, Ashu Priestly Ojong,[^cite_note-230] and hours later the chief of the Babeke Telecentre, Kimi Samson, was murdered as well by suspected separatists.[^cite_note-231] Tribal clashes took place in Ndu, where armed Fulani killed two civilians.[^cite_note-232]

On 19 May, one of the imprisoned members of the original cabinet of the IG, Barrister Shufai, who had been transferred to a hospital four days prior, was chained up and mistreated by guards. Shufai had been transferred to the hospital following 10 days of deteriorating health, and was unconscious when he arrived.[^cite_note-233] He was then sent back to [Kondengui Central Prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kondengui_Central_Prison), despite the fact that he was immunocompromised and the risk of getting [COVID-19](63030231.md) in a crowded prison. His treatment was condemned by his lawyers and by [Human Rights Watch](46530.md).[^cite_note-234]

The next day, Cameroon celebrated its annual [Unity Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Day_\(Cameroon\)). Separatists had already announced a lockdown from 19 to 21 May, and fighters patrolled public places during the holiday to enforce the lockdown.[^cite_note-235] According to the Cameroonian military, six people were killed in clashes in the preceding week. Separatists claimed to have abducted nine or more people who had watched the celebrations on TV.[^cite_note-236] In Bamenda, the Cameroonian Army claimed to have discovered and disarmed several [improvised explosive devices](265112.md).[^cite_note-237]

On 24 May, the Cameroonian military set up a military base in Ngarbuh, with the stated aim of cutting off a separatist supply route from Nigeria. Within a week, more than 300 villagers had fled, fearing the presence of soldiers and a repeat of the Ngarbuh massacre.[^cite_note-238] Four days later, Cameroonian soldiers killed four unarmed men in Buea.[^cite_note-239] Another person was killed by soldiers in [Mbiame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mbiame).[^cite_note-240] Towards the end of the month, separatists drew condemnation from Human Rights Watch for abducting and maltreating a humanitarian worker and members of a non-profit organization. All were eventually released.[^cite_note-241]

On 1 June, at least five Cameroonian soldiers were killed in a separatist ambush in Otu, [Manyu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manyu_\(department\)). An unspecified number of soldiers were also wounded in the ambush.[^cite_note-242] The next day, it was reported that Pidgin journalist Samuel Wazizi, who had been arrested on 3 August 2019 over alleged ties with separatists, had died at a military hospital after being tortured by Cameroonian soldiers.[^cite_note-243] Cameroonian authorities confirmed Wazizi's death three days after the initial reports, reiterated the original accusation against Wazizi, and attributed his death to "severe [sepsis](158400.md)".[^cite_note-244] In Malende, Kumba, the Cameroonian Army carried out an operation to expel separatists from the area. According to Cameroon, seven separatists were killed as the Cameroonian Army overran their base.[^cite_note-245] On 5 June, separatist fighters killed a gendarmerie commander in [Njikwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Njikwa).[^cite_note-246] In mid-June, the Cameroonian Army launched two separate operations that killed a total of 24 separatists over four days; 11 in Mbokam, and 13 in Bali, Batibo and Widikum. Cameroon claimed to have suffered one dead and one wounded during the battles.[^cite_note-macaub18jun020-247] During the four days of fighting, the Cameroonian Army lifted a blockade of the Bamenda-[Enugu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enugu_\(city\)) Road, where rebels had extorted money from travellers. Separatists condemned the blockade and denied having anything to do with it.[^cite_note-248]

In June, Cameroon planned to start rebuilding the Anglophone regions with the support of the [UNDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Development_Programme). In response, separatists declared a three-day lockdown all across the Anglophone regions.[^cite_note-249] Meanwhile, two explosive devices detonated in [Yaoundé](57671.md), raising suspicions that the separatists were now taking the war to Cameroon's capital; the Cameroonian police had warned about this back in January.[^cite_note-250] Another explosion in the beginning of July wounded two people.[^cite_note-251] Following the explosions, security forces started raiding houses in neighborhoods where displaced Anglophones were living. In the following days, many Anglophones accused the authorities of abuses.[^cite_note-252] In early July, Cameroon launched "Operation Ngoke-Bui", a series of raids on separatist camps in [Ngo-Ketunjia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngo-Ketunjia), in which 17 separatists and at least one Cameroonian soldier were killed.[^cite_note-253]

By the end of June, *[The Africa Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Africa_Report)* reported that Cameroon had largely pushed the separatists out of Buea (although they later clashed with the army there in early-July[^cite_note-254]), where there had been much fighting back in 2018. Separatist militias in Northwest Region suffered heavy losses during operations in June, and a source in the Cameroonian military claimed that Cameroon was close to winning the war.[^cite_note-theafricanreportjun252020-255] Other sources claimed that neither side was winning, and that internal discontent was forcing Cameroon towards a ceasefire. International calls for a global ceasefire to deal with the ongoing COVID-19 pandemic also put pressure on the Cameroonian government.[^cite_note-reutersjul42020-18] On 2 July, Cameroonian officials met with the imprisoned leaders of the IG to discuss a ceasefire.[^cite_note-256] Ayuk Tabe listed three conditions for a ceasefire; that it would be announced by President Biya, that the Cameroonian military would pull out of the Anglophone regions, and a general amnesty for separatists.[^cite_note-theafricareportjul62020-19] According to an article published in [The New Humanitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Humanitarian) "The two rounds of "pre-talks" – the last reportedly on 2 July – have been held outside the Kondengui Central Prison where Sisiku is being held and are seen as "confidence-building" steps."[^cite_note-thenewhumanitarian.org-20] These talks were condemned by other factions of the Ambazonia independence movement, who argued that Ayuk Tabe had been corrupted by the Cameroonian government.[^cite_note-thenewhumanitarian.org-20] Around the same time, separatists imposed a three-day lockdown in response to Cameroonian plans to begin rebuilding the Anglophone regions with the support of the [UNDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Development_Programme), titled "Presidential Programme for the Reconstruction and Development" (PPRD).[^cite_note-257] Heavy fighting took place in June, with the separatists suffering dozens of casualties.[^cite_note-macaub18jun020-247]

On 13 July, a separatist militia known as the "Gorilla Fighters"[^cite_note-cnaoct132020-258] led by "General Ayeke" abducted 63 civilians from [Mmuock Leteh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mmuock_Leteh), Lebialem. 25 of them were released the next day, and the rest were released on 15 July. At least some of the civilians paid ransom to secure their release. One anonymous hostage said that they had been maltreated.[^cite_note-259] Between 13 and 19 July, the Cameroonian Army carried out raids in Awing and Pinyin, killing at least 15 separatist fighters, including a general known as "General Okoro". Another separatist general, "Bush General", was taken prisoner.

On 14 August, villagers killed 17 separatist fighters in Nguti, [Koupé-Manengouba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koup%C3%A9-Manengouba). A local separatist leader identified those killed as rogue separatists.[^cite_note-260] On 19 August, it was reported that more than 130 ex-separatist fighters had escaped from re-integration centers. According to local newspapers, a number had re-joined the separatist ranks.[^cite_note-261] Taking steps to stop the separatists from producing their own weapons, the authorities banned the sale of machetes and iron rods in the Anglophone regions.[^cite_note-262]

September saw heavy escalation in Bamenda. Separatists killed a police officer on 1 September,[^cite_note-263] Police forces responded by destroying shops and shooting indiscriminately, forcing people to stay indoors.[^cite_note-264] In the following days, hundreds of people were detained, and locals accused the military of atrocities.[^cite_note-265] Elsewhere in Bamenda, separatist fighters killed a police officer.[^cite_note-266] and four days later, Cameroonian troops killed Ambazonian general Luca Fonteh in the city.[^cite_note-267] On 8 September, Cameroon announced [Operation Bamenda Clean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Bamenda_Clean) to weed out separatists from the city;[^cite_note-268] the announcement was followed shortly by civilians accusing soldiers of extortion.[^cite_note-269] Cameroonian soldiers summarily executed two people on 14 September,[^cite_note-270] and another five civilians days later.[^cite_note-cnasep222020-271] On 22 September, Cameroonian soldiers killed six civilians in Buea, allegedly for not showing them the locations of separatist hideouts.[^cite_note-cnasep222020-271] Another civilian was killed in Bui four days later.[^cite_note-272]

On 28 September, Cameroon announced that over the last four days it had expelled separatist fighters from at least a hundred schools across the Anglophone regions, with the aim of reopening schools on 5 October. At least nine separatist fighters and at least two Cameroonian soldiers died during the fighting.[^cite_note-273]

1 October marked the three-year anniversary of Ambazonia's declaration of independence. To commemorate the occasion, separatist fighters raised flags in several areas in the Anglophone regions, including in Buea. Celebrations were also held in Lebialem.[^cite_note-274] Anglophone refugees in Nigeria also celebrated the independence day.[^cite_note-275] Cameroon sent reinforcements to stop the celebrations, and clashes were reported in Bui, Momo and Boyo.[^cite_note-crisisgroupoct2020-276] On 11 October, Cameroon initiated a three-day offensive against separatists in [Wabane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wabane),[^cite_note-jdcoct192020-277] Lebialem.[^cite_note-xinhuaoct142020-278] Two days later, it was revealed that Cameroonian forces had killed "General Ayeke", commander of the "Gorilla Fighters" militia, in Besali, Wabane, Lebialem.[^cite_note-cnaoct132020-258] Some 13 hostages were freed from the separatist camp, which was subsequently destroyed. One Cameroonian soldier was killed during the same incident, and the operation was described as "bloody", although the other separatist fighters at the base were reported to have escaped. The corpse of "General Ayeke" was flown to Kumba where it was publicly displayed.[^cite_note-279] In total, three Cameroonian soldiers and at least 12 separatists died during the three-day offensive.[^cite_note-xinhuaoct142020-278]

On 24 October, the [Kumba school massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumba_school_massacre) took place,[^cite_note-aljazeeraoct242020-280] with 10–12 gunmen killing 7 children and wounding another 13.[^cite_note-jdcoct30202-281] No one claimed responsibility for the attack, and the Cameroonian government and the separatist movements blamed each other.[^cite_note-aljazeeraoct242020-280] Days later, the Cameroonian government announced that the BIR had identified and killed a separatist commander who was responsible for the massacre.[^cite_note-282]

On 4 December, separatist forces started enforcing a four-day lockdown aimed at disrupting the 6 December regional election.[^cite_note-283] Throughout election day, there were clashes in Bamenda. A number of people were shot, and one CPDM Councilor was killed after getting caught in the crossfire during a shootout between separatist fighters and police forces.[^cite_note-284] In Kumbo, separatist fighters entered a church and held the occupants hostage. There were also clashes in Buea.[^cite_note-cnadec62020-285] In Babessi, a separatist fighter was ambushed and killed in his home by soldiers.[^cite_note-286]

In an end-of-year speech, Samuel Ikome Sako of the Interim Government of Ambazonia declared that an All Ambazonia Constitutional Draft Conference would be held during 2021, and that Northeast and Southwest Region would be reorganized into Equatorial Zone, Midland Zone and Savanna Zone. Sako also said that the separatists were purchasing more weapons and that the fight would continue.[^cite_note-287] Meanwhile, Biya thanked the population of the Anglophone regions for assisting the government in the fight against separatists, and called on other countries to crack down on separatist activities in the diaspora.[^cite_note-288]

### 2021

Main article: [Timeline of the Anglophone Crisis (2021)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Anglophone_Crisis_\(2021\))

On 6 January, separatist fighters ambushed the convoy of the Senior Divisional Officer of Momo near Njikwa, using [improvised explosive devices](265112.md).[^cite_note-289] Five soldiers and one civilian were killed.[^cite_note-290] Two days later, separatists killed three gendarme officers, one policeman and two civilians at a checkpoint in Matazem. Four others were injured.[^cite_note-291]

In mid-January, separatists moved to sabotage the postponed [2020 African Nations Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_African_Nations_Championship) in Limbe. On 14 January, explosions were heard outside Limbe Omnisport Stadium. A separatist militia called the "Fako Action Forces" claimed responsibility.[^cite_note-292] The next day, suspected separatists burned a beer truck in Likomba, Fako Division. Security forces pursued the attackers, and an ensuing shootout left two suspected separatists dead.[^cite_note-293]

In the early months of 2021, the separatists began to rely heavily on IEDs while carrying out their ambushes, resulting in heavier Cameroonian casualties.[^cite_note-aljazeeraapr12021-10] On 18 February, seven Cameroonian soldiers were killed by an IED in Babessi.[^cite_note-crisisgroupfeb2021-294] On 24 February, at least two soldiers were killed and at least another five were wounded[^cite_note-295] in a separatist ambush in Kumbo with IEDs followed by gunfire.[^cite_note-296] Following the incident, soldiers went on a shooting spree while searching for the attackers.[^cite_note-297] In Bui, Cameroon launched "Operation Bui 1", which resulted in the deaths of 12 separatists and two Cameroonian soldiers; days later, a separatist militia known as the Bui Warriors launched a series of IED attacks against Cameroonian soldiers in Bui, followed by an attack on a prison. The Bui Warriors claimed to have killed at least five soldiers with the IED attacks alone.[^cite_note-298] Three BIR soldiers were wounded when their vehicle struck an IED between Kumbo and Ndop on 10 April.[^cite_note-299] The Red Dragon militia successfully ambushed a military vehicle in Alou with an IED on 24 April,[^cite_note-300] while the Seven Karta militia carried out a similar IED attack in Bafut.[^cite_note-301] On 29 April, the "Bambalang Marine Forces" carried out a cross-border raid into Ngalim, West Region, killing four soldiers and taking off with several weapons, suffering no losses on their own.[^cite_note-302] A month later, the same militia killed five soldiers in an attack on an outpost in Bui,[^cite_note-xinhua250521-303] two weeks into a separatist offensive against Cameroon in the area.[^cite_note-304]

On 9 April, the AGovC formally allied itself with the [Indigenous People of Biafra](49020788.md), led by [Nnamdi Kanu](48322622.md).[^cite_note-cnaapr92021-305] According to ADF deputy defense chief Daniel Caapo, this would entail joint military operations, joint training bases, and an effort to seize the mutual border and ensure a free flow of weapons and personnel.[^cite_note-foreignpolicymay202021-306] The ADF offered to train IPOB militants at camps in Ambazonian-controlled territory.[^cite_note-307] [Ayaba Cho Lucas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayaba_Cho_Lucas) also proposed an alliance with democratic forces within Cameroon, suggesting that Ambazonia should help overthrow Paul Biya.[^cite_note-308] The AGovC-IPOB alliance was denounced by the Interim Government of Ambazonia as well as by other Biafran separatist groups.[^cite_note-309]

On 4 May, separatists carried out two ambushes with IEDs; in Akwaya, causing unknown casualties,[^cite_note-310] and in Ekona.[^cite_note-311] Three civilians were killed when a bus hit an IED in Sabga on 9 May; separatists claimed responsibility, and said that the IED had been meant for militar targets.[^cite_note-312] In mid-May, the "Fako Mountain Lions" invaded Muyuka and Muea and essentially dared the Cameroonian Army to attack; the subsequent battle saw the deaths of two Cameroonian soldiers.[^cite_note-313]

In mid-May, separatists launched an offensive in Bui against the Cameroonian Army, which in turn retaliated by burning down civilian homes in several villages.[^cite_note-314] Over the weekend 23–25 May, more than 25 Cameroonian soldiers were killed and more than 12 military vehicles were damaged in Kumbo, Bui.[^cite_note-cnafbmay252021-315] On 24 May, the Cameroonian Army claimed to have killed separatist commander "General Akwaba" and an unspecified number of fighters in a raid on their camp in Kumbo.[^cite_note-cnafbmay252021-315] The next day, five Cameroonian soldiers, including a brigade commander,[^cite_note-fbcnamay252021-316] were killed when separatist militia the Bambalang Marine Forces attacked an army outpost in Noni in Bui. The separatists also seized weapons and equipment.[^cite_note-xinhua250521-303] In Ekona, the Fako Mountain Lions carried out three IED attacks against the Cameroonian army, and three civilians were killed in the subsequent gunfights.[^cite_note-317] In [Jakiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakiri), Bui, Cameroonian soldiers arrested three people and subsequently murdered them.[^cite_note-318]

### 2022

Main article: [Timeline of the Anglophone Crisis (2022)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Anglophone_Crisis_\(2022\))

On 3 January, separatists detonated an [improvised explosive device](265112.md) (IED) in the town of [Limbe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limbe,_Cameroon), [Southwest Region](3028638.md), which was set to host matches of the delayed [2021 Africa Cup of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Africa_Cup_of_Nations) (AFCON). Separatist fighters who had vowed to disrupt 2021 Africa Cup of Nations in the city said on social media they were responsible for the blast and stated that it was "a warning sign of what they will do during AFCON".[^cite_note-china.org-319]

The delayed AFCON is scheduled to be held in Limbe and [Buea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buea) starting on 9 January. Cameroon has deployed additional troops to the cities, while separatists have warned the [Confederation of African Football](715265.md) against holding the tournament.[^cite_note-voajan52022-320]

On 12 January, The [Mali national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mali_national_football_team) suspended [training](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Training) before the AFCON game against [Tunisia](30188.md) following [clashes](2296086.md) between separatists and the [Cameroon Armed Forces](5455.md) in [Buea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buea), killing two.[^cite_note-321] Three police officers were also injured by a homemade bomb.[^cite_note-322] In a separate incident near Buea, a Cameroonian soldier was killed in a separatist IED ambush; the [Ambazonia Defence Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambazonia_Defence_Forces) (ADF) claimed responsibility.[^cite_note-323] The [Tunisian Football Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunisian_Football_Federation) decided to cancel the post-game recovery session of the [Tunisia national football team](1029953.md) due to "terrorist threats".[^cite_note-kawarji.com-324]

On 31 January, a separatist militia known as the "Bui Warriors" battled Cameroonian forces in Bamkikai, [Kumbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumbo), until the Cameroonian Army withdrew from the area. At least three Cameroonian soldiers were reportedly killed, and one military vehicle was destroyed.[^cite_note-325]

On 2 March, seven people including a Divisional Officer, and a mayor were killed in a separatist IED ambush in [Ekondo-Titi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ekondo-Titi), Southwest Region. The ADF claimed responsibility.[^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327]

On 5 April, separatists attacked the [University of Bamenda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Bamenda) for defying a [ghost town](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghost_town) operation.[^cite_note-328] In Manyu, at least seven Cameroonian soldiers were killed and another seven were wounded in a separatist attack on four [military checkpoints](8116691.md).[^cite_note-cnaapr142022-329]

On 6 April, separatists abducted alleged anti-separatists protesters in Oku, Northwest Region.[^cite_note-330] Separatists set ablaze an unspecified number of houses who reportedly belongs to the Mbororos people in [Ndu](25460446.md), Northwest Region.[^cite_note-331] 33 [seminarians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seminary) were kidnapped for ransom by separatists in [Manyu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manyu_\(department\)), [Mamfe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamfe), Southwest Region then released 24 hours after their kidnapping.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333] Four Cameroonian soldiers were killed and another six were wounded in a separatist IED ambush in Mamfe.[^cite_note-cnaapr142022-329]

On 20 May, multiple clashes between the Cameroon Army and separatists took place in the English-speaking regions of Cameroon during the [National Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Day_\(Cameroon\)). The Cameroonian Army claimed at least 28 separatists were killed during the battles, while the ADF claimed that 24 Cameroonian soldiers were killed.[^cite_note-334]

On 29 May, separatist forces invaded the villages of Obonyi and Kajifu in [Akwaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akwaya), near the Nigerian border, reportedly with the aim of abducting selected individuals. Clashes ensued, and many villagers fled across the border into the villages of Bashu and Danare in [Boki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boki,_Nigeria), [Cross River State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross_River_State). The separatists pursued them into Nigerian territory and killed at least 20 people, injured another 70, and burned down dozens of houses. The invasion was condemned by the [Biafra Nations League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biafra_Nations_League) (BNL), a [Biafran](44431.md) separatist group with otherwise friendly relations with the Ambazonian movement. The BNL threatened to respond unless Ambazonian leaders condemned the invasion.[^cite_note-335][^cite_note-336] In Batibo, Cameroonian Army freed several hostages held by the ADF, including Senator Regina Mundi who had been held since 30 April. The army said that it had killed ten separatists and captured three.[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338] The ADF denied suffering any casualties, and said that the fighters had released the hostages in order to escape in time. The ADF also alleged that the Cameroonian Army had abused civilians during their search for the senator.[^cite_note-339] In Kumbo, Cameroonian soldiers killed five people.[^cite_note-340]

On 7 June, between five and nine Cameroonian gendarmes were killed and another three were wounded in a separatist attack on a checkpoint in Njitapon, [Kouoptamo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kouoptamo), [West Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Region_\(Cameroon\)). The attack was led by [General No Pity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_No_Pity).[^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342] More than a hundred fighters participated in the attack, and they arrived in flying boats with the engines shut off in order not to make noise. The separatists then blew up the checkpoint with grenades, and used a rocket launcher to destroy army vehicles. Cameroonian Army reinforcements failed to arrive before the separatists had already withdrawn back across the river.[^cite_note-343]

On 25–26 June, the BIR carried out into the villages of Banten, Mborshia, and Yere in Bui division, during which they ambushed an ADF hideout and killed between two and five fighters. The ADF claimed that two Cameroonian soldiers were also killed.[^cite_note-344]

On 12 July, Ambazonian Field Marshall [Lekeaka Oliver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lekeaka_Oliver), leader of the [Red Dragons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Dragon_\(Ambazonian_militia\)), was killed in [Menji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menji). Cameroonian forces were able to retrieve the body three days later.
