According to the Cameroonian government, Oliver and one of his guards had been killed in a Cameroonian raid.[^cite_note-345] Separatists claimed that Oliver had been killed by an insider during separatist infighting; his assassin had revealed the location of the body to Cameroonian forces.[^cite_note-346]

On 31 July, Cameroonian forces initiated a three-day, multi-front attack on the ADF in the Batibo district. Two separatist fighters known as "Colonel John" and "General Rasta" including 15 other ADF fighters were killed by the Cameroonian forces in [Bambui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bambui), and another 18 were said to have been captured. The Cameroonian Army said that eleven of its forces were injured during the operation, while the ADF claimed to have killed "at least two dozen" soldiers. Local eyewitnesses reported losses on both sides, as well as civilian deaths.[^cite_note-347] The ADF confirmed the death of eleven of their men,[^cite_note-348] and said that they had cancelled an offensive in Bamenda involving 300 fighters due to the losses.[^cite_note-349] In Bwabwa, six civilians were abducted by separatists.[^cite_note-350]

On 28 August, multiple separatist attacks occurred in at least three different localities across the Anglophone regions. Three soldiers were killed and many others injured in an IED ambush led by [Seven Karta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Karta) in Bafut. In Ngokuv, unknown casualties after the Bui Unity Warriors fired a [rocket](26301.md) at an armored car. In [Eyumodjock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eyumodjock), a gendarmerie brigade was attacked by separatists; the gendarmes escaped before the attack occurred.[^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352]

### 2023

Main article: [Timeline of the Anglophone Crisis (2023)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Anglophone_Crisis_\(2023\))

On 1–2 January, following President [Paul Biya](392319.md)'s announcement in his New Years' speech that the Cameroonian military was crushing the Ambazonian rebellion, separatists moved to enforce local lockdowns. The [Cameroon Armed Forces](5455.md) announced that it had deployed scores of troops to [Oku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oku,_Cameroon), [Kumbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumbo) and [Jakiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakiri), [Northwest Region](646814.md), and claimed to have killed 11 separatists. The [Ambazonia Defence Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambazonia_Defence_Forces) (ADF) announced that the separatists had expanded their territorial grip and that the war would continue.[^cite_note-voajan22023-353]

On 1–3 February, separatists carried out a string of attacks in Bamessing, Oku, Nkambe, Kumbo, Kom and Nkambe, using [improvised explosive devices](265112.md) (IEDs). The Cameroonian military said that several civilians were killed or abducted, while some military vehicles were also destroyed. Ambazonia Restoration Forces commander Sagon Jaguar said in a video that his forces had destroyed five military vehicles in Bamessing using IEDs.[^cite_note-354] On 25 February, nineteen athletes were wounded in multiple explosions during the [Mount Cameroon Race of Hope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Cameroon_Race_of_Hope). The Ambazonia Defence Forces (ADF) claimed responsibility, saying that their primary target had been Cameroonian elite forces.[^cite_note-355]

In the days leading up to the 12 March [Cameroon senatorial election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Cameroon_senatorial_election), separatists blocked roads and carried out a string of lethal attacks against military vehicles. Battles were recorded in Tadu, Bamenda, Ndop, Wum, Jakiri, Oku, Bambili, Sabga, Mamfe, Menji and Tiko. The Cameroonian Army said that two dozen separatists had been killed, while acknowledging the loss of several military vehicles. The SDF said that the clashes had made it impossible for its candidates to campaign.[^cite_note-356]

On 1 May, separatist fighters attacked a checkpoint in Matouke, [Littoral Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Littoral_Region_\(Cameroon\)), killing five soldiers or police officers and one civilian, and wounding many others. The separatists seized soldiers' clothing, weapons and ammunition before heading back into bordering [Southwest Region](3028638.md). A small group of separatists from Ambazonia Liberation Council claimed responsibility.[^cite_note-357] The attack was regarded as notable because Matouke is located less than 40 kilometers from [Douala](19997263.md), the economic capital of Cameroon.[^cite_note-358] In Mamu near Ekona, [Muyuka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muyuka), at least six people were killed and more were wounded in a raid by the Cameroonian Army.[^cite_note-359]

On 24 May, it was reported that separatists had abducted 30 women in Babanki days prior. Separatist activist Capo Daniel confirmed the abduction, stating that the women were being punished for "allowing Cameroon's administration to manipulate them".[^cite_note-360] All the women were released the same day as the incident was first reported, many with serious injuries; a local source said that some had gunfire wounds and that one might require an amputation.[^cite_note-361]

On 16 July, gunmen killed ten people and injured another two at a busy junction in Bamenda.[^cite_note-362] Seven of the dead were from [West Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Region_\(Cameroon\)).[^cite_note-363] Without claiming responsibility for the attack, [Ayaba Cho Lucas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayaba_Cho_Lucas) declared that henceforth, whenever Cameroonian forces killed Anglophone civilians, the ADF would retaliate by killing civilians from the Francophone parts of Cameroon.[^cite_note-364]

On 6 November, separatist fighters carried out the [Egbekaw massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egbekaw_massacre), killing at least 20 civilians, injuring another 10, and setting some 15 houses on fire in the Egbekaw, near Mamfe.[^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366] Local sources reported more than 30 dead, many of whom were burned alive in their sleep. At least 15 people were abducted, and an unspecified number were raped. *Cameroon News Agency* reported that a group known as the "Tigers of Manyu" was responsible for the attack, and had carried it out as revenge for the death of one of their fighters two days prior.[^cite_note-cnanov62023-367] The perpetrators were reported to be natives of Egbekaw.[^cite_note-368]

On 5 December, Ambazonian separatists reportedly took control of the Belegete community inside Nigeria (in [Obanliku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obanliku)), and abducted some 30 people including traditional leader Chief Francis Ogweshi. Two days later, Nigerian troops attempted a rescue mission in the jungle, during which an Ambazonian fighter was killed. The Ambazonians responded by killing the chief, whose corpse was discovered three days later. Ambazonian forces carried out subsequent attacks in Belegete, where they burned houses and attacked civilians with guns and cutlasses.[^cite_note-369] Nigerian forces were later deployed to oust the Ambazonians, but they were unable to reach Belegete with a sizeable force due to the topography and the poor infrastructure, and camped at the [Obudu Mountain Resort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obudu_Mountain_Resort).[^cite_note-370] The rebels later retreated from Belegete on their own, but threatened that they would return at a later date.[^cite_note-371]

### 2024

Main article: [Timeline of the Anglophone Crisis (2024)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Anglophone_Crisis_\(2024\))

On 1 January, writing from the [Kondengui Central Prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kondengui_Central_Prison), Sisiku Julius Ayuk Tabe (the first president of Ambazonia) called for an end of the [Ambazonian leadership crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambazonian_leadership_crisis) and unity among separatist factions.[^cite_note-\:03-372] On 6 January, footage surfaced in which armed men claiming to be part of the Ambazonia Defence Forces (ADF) were torturing a civilian in Muyuka.[^cite_note-\:1-373] On 9 January, Cameroonian forces raided a separatist hideout in Ewai. Cameroon claimed that at least five separatists were killed, while the separatists claimed to have suffered three dead. Hundreds of people fled Ewai during the raid.[^cite_note-\:2-374] On 11 January, a gendarme officer shot a civilian dead in Mamfe.[^cite_note-375] On 13 January, separatist fighters shot and tortured a civilian in Menchum, after accusing him of passing information to the Cameroonian military.[^cite_note-376] On 21 January, it was confirmed that Cameroonian forces had captured top ADF commander "General Efang".[^cite_note-377] On 24 January, suspected separatists burned a road construction equipment in [Ndu](25460446.md).[^cite_note-378] On 29 January, several people were feared dead and three vehicles were burned in a separatist attack in [Buea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buea).[^cite_note-379]

On 6 February, an official was abducted by unidentified kidnappers in Bamenda. He was rescued four hours later by the Cameroonian military.[^cite_note-380] The kidnapping was attributed to the Red Dragon militia.[^cite_note-381] On 7 February, four civilians were killed by Cameroonian forces in Kumbo.[^cite_note-382] On 11 February, one person was killed and dozens more were injured, including children, in an explosion during Youth Day celebrations in Nkambe.[^cite_note-383] On 16–17 February, eight separatists were killed during military operations in the locality of Ayukaba.[^cite_note-384] Two persons were killed during clashes in Banga Bakundu.[^cite_note-cnafeb222024-385] On 21 February, one person was killed during clashes in Banga Bakundu.[^cite_note-cnafeb222024-385] On 26 February, Cameroonian security forces detected and deactivated an improvised explosive device (IED) in Galim, West Region. According to local sources, separatists meant to target the Divisional Officer of Galim.[^cite_note-386]

On 9–11 March, a separatist commander known as Barack was killed by the Rapid Intervention Brigade during clashes near Batibo.[^cite_note-387] On 12 March, two civilians were killed in a suspected separatist attack on a Brasseries du Cameroun truck in Bamenda.[^cite_note-388] On 22–23 March, a local elected official was shot dead in [Babessi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babessi), in an attack claimed by separatists.[^cite_note-389] On 25 March, the Cameroonian military said it had killed at least seven separatist fighters in Tubah.[^cite_note-390] On 27 March, Cameroonian forces carried out a series of raids against separatist hideouts in Big Babanki, Bamenda and Bambui. 13 separatist fighters were reported killed in Bambui, and "several" were killed in Big Babanki. Two separatist fighters were also killed in Jakiri.[^cite_note-391] On 31 March, a separatist group called the Ribbons of Ambazonia announced that they had banned farming on Mondays in the Meme and Ndian divisions.[^cite_note-392]

On 4 April, a severed head was discovered at the Azire Old Church Junction in Bamenda. Witnessed said that it was dropped there by separatist fighters.[^cite_note-393] On 10 April, a [battle took place in Kumbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kumbo): At night, a group of rebels stormed Kumbo and attacked the local BIR base, resulting in several deaths.[^cite_note-Mbinkar-394] The attack was reportedly carried out by the Bui Unity Warriors. Afterward, Cameroonian forces organized revenge raids in the area, set ablaze houses in three villages of the Bui Division, and killed three civilians and one separatist fighter in Bamfem.[^cite_note-395] On 15 April, separatist fighters abducted two teachers in Njap, Donga-Mantung.[^cite_note-396] On 24 April, separatist general Desmond Koa (known as "General Mad Dog") was killed along with four of his fighters by the Rapid Intervention Brigade in Mbonge, Ndian.[^cite_note-397] On 29 April, three men were reportedly killed and several others injured in an alleged separatist attack in Kumba.[^cite_note-398] A locally made bomb exploded in Nkwen, Bamenda, causing no casualties. The attack was claimed by ADF leader, Ayaba Cho Lucas.[^cite_note-399] Separatist fighters set fire to a bulldozer working on the Tugi-Bamenda road. A commercial motorcycle was also seized and set on fire by the fighters for violating ghost town.[^cite_note-400]

On 1 May, separatist fighters abducted 28 people (including eight children) from the Catholic Church of Bai Panya in Meme. A group calling itself the "Al-Qaeda Squad of Meme", led by "Field Marshall Lucifer", claimed responsibility. The group accused the abducted civilians of attempting to get birth certificates for the upcoming [2025 Cameroonian presidential election](73432126.md), and threatened to summarily execute them.[^cite_note-mimimefomay132024-401] On 4 May, former ADF spokesperson and Ambazonia People's Rights Advocacy Group President [Capo Daniel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capo_Daniel) called for an end of the armed struggle against Cameroon. The communiqué called for negotiations and a switch to [nonviolent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonviolence) resistance, and for fighters to only use their weapons defensively pending an agreement.[^cite_note-402] On 5 May, a separatist commander known as General Sagon was killed by Cameroonian forces along with two of his collaborators in [Bamunka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamunka), Ndop.[^cite_note-403] Three days later, separatists blocked the Bamenda-Ndop road in retaliation.[^cite_note-404] On 7 May, the Black Lions of Bamali seized a gendarmerie truck in Ndop. The action was led by "General Kolambo".[^cite_note-405] On 8 May, separatist fighters led by "Field Marshall Lucifer" captured and burned down a military post in Bomana, Meme.[^cite_note-406] On 10 May, separatists killed six gendarmes in Eyumodjock, including a commander, and seized weapons and uniforms.[^cite_note-407][^cite_note-408] On 12 May, a soldier from the Rapid Intervention Brigade reportedly shot at civilians in a Limbe nightclub, killing one and wounding two, in revenge for the killings of his fellow soldiers in Bamenda by separatist fighters.[^cite_note-409] On 14 May, suspected separatist fighters raided a school in Ndu and abducted two teachers.[^cite_note-410] Four people including two government forces and two civilians were killed in a separatist attack in Bambui.[^cite_note-411] On 15 May, ADF fighters attacked a school and burned materials.[^cite_note-412] On 16 May, a brigade commander and a gendarme were killed in a separatist attack in Akwaya, Manyu.[^cite_note-413] On 17 May, four separatist fighters were killed during a government offensive in Mbveh and Ndzenji, Bui.[^cite_note-414] On 19 May, clashes between separatist fighters and Cameroonian forces left three people dead, including a separatist, a soldier and a young child in Akum, Mezam.[^cite_note-415] On 20 May, during Cameroon's National Day, separatist fighters killed the mayor and two other people in Belo. Another attack took place in Njinkom.[^cite_note-416] Separatist fighters killed a soldier in [Mundemba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mundemba).[^cite_note-417] On 24 May, the Nigerian Army arrested eight men in Taraba State, on the border between Nigeria and Cameroon, for smuggling fuel to Ambazonian rebels.[^cite_note-418] On 25 May, two people were killed and eleven seriously injured in an attack on a bar in Bamenda.[^cite_note-419] On 28 May, a student and a policeman were injured in Bamenda during an attempt by separatists to disrupt the written part of the GCE exam.[^cite_note-420] In Olu, Bui, separatist fighters launched an attack, killing one student and wounding others.[^cite_note-421] On 29 May, a former Red Dragon fighter who had switched sides to fight for the government was killed in a suspected planned assassination.[^cite_note-422]

On 1 June, separatist fighters killed an official in a roadside ambush in Donga-Mantung.[^cite_note-423] On 5 June, suspected separatist fighters killed a councillor in an ambush in Menchum.[^cite_note-424] Separatists burned a taxi at a former hospital in Bamenda.[^cite_note-425] On 8 June, armed men claiming to be ADF fighters set fire to a vehicle in Bamenda.[^cite_note-426] On 10 June, suspected separatist fighters killed a bike rider and abducted a passenger in Bamenda.[^cite_note-427] On 13 June, separatist commander Besaka Belget (known as "General Black Bat") was killed by Cameroonian forces in Kombone Bakundu, Meme.[^cite_note-428] On 16 June, a former separatist fighter was killed by ADF in Bamenda.[^cite_note-429] On 17 June, a nurse was killed by ADF fighters in Bafut.[^cite_note-430] At least two people were reportedly injured in a separatist gunfire in Buea. Locals said the attackers identified themselves as the Mountain Lions of Fako.[^cite_note-431] On 18 June, the ADF set fire to a cab in Bamenda.[^cite_note-432] On 21 June, it was reported that Cameroonian forces had used civilians in Melim to search for IEDs, resulting in the deaths of two civilians.[^cite_note-433] On 26 June, three civilians were killed in an IED attack in Oshum, Batibo.[^cite_note-434] ADF claimed responsibility.[^cite_note-435] On 30 June, two separatist fighters, including an ADF commander known as "General Stone", were killed during an operation by security forces in Bamenda. Their bodies were subsequently burned.[^cite_note-436][^cite_note-437]

On 1 July, two soldiers were killed in Munyenge, Muyuka.[^cite_note-438] In Tadu, Bui, a soldier was killed and his weapon seized by separatist fighters.[^cite_note-439] On 4 July, separatist fighters killed a man and abducted his son in Ntumbaw, Ndu, Donga-Mantung.[^cite_note-440] On 7 July, Cameroonian forces reportedly invaded Bamali, killing two civilians and injuring others.[^cite_note-441] On 8 July, a retired soldier and an [internally displaced person](427408.md) was killed by suspected separatists in Bamenda.[^cite_note-442] On 10 July, two separatist fighters, including a leader known as ''Commander Iron'', were killed during a military operation in Bamenda.[^cite_note-443] On 14 July, separatist fighters killed a young man and abducted his brother in Bamenda.[^cite_note-444] On 15 July, suspected separatists set fire to the palace of Chief Ewome John Eko in Bwassa, Southwest Region, resulting in the destruction of one section of the complex. The attackers left a note with a warning for collaborators, along with an Ambazonian flag.[^cite_note-445] Separatists burned three bikes in Ngie, Momo.[^cite_note-446] On 19 July, at least four armed separatists, including a deputy commander, were killed in a military offensive in Alabukam, Bamenda.[^cite_note-447] On 21 July, the ADF attacked the office of the Divisional Officer in Jakiri, Bui.[^cite_note-448] On 23 July, a single mother was killed by suspected separatist fighters in Bamenda.[^cite_note-449] Separatist fighters attacked Mbessa, Boyo, killing one villager, kidnapping and wounding others.[^cite_note-450] On 28 July, separatist fighters shot and wounded the deputy mayor of Oku.[^cite_note-451] On 30 July, Fulani militants attacked the village of Sop in Ndu, burning several homes and killing at least one person.[^cite_note-452]

On 7 August, two police officers were killed and their weapon seized by separatist fighters in Bamenda. The ADF claimed responsibility.[^cite_note-453] It was reported that Fulani militiamen invaded once again the village of Sop in Ndu setting fire to houses, looting stores and attacking villagers, leaving many wounded.[^cite_note-454] On 9 August, two police officers were killed in a bomb explosion in Bamenda.[^cite_note-455] On 10 August, following the deaths of two separatist fighters in [Bangulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangulan), Ngo-Ketunjia, separatist commander "General No Mercy" declared an indefinite lockdown in the village.[^cite_note-456] On 14 August, five cabs were set on fire by unidentified gunmen in Bamenda.[^cite_note-457] On 16 August, separatist fighters imposed a blockade on the Kumba-Mamfe road.[^cite_note-458] In Bui, a police inspector was killed in a separatist ambush.[^cite_note-459] On 19 August, it was reported that Cameroonian forces carried out a combined ambush and targeting operation between Ndop and Bamessing, Ngo Kentunjia. During this operation, two key members of the "[Only Bro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_The_Only_Bro)" group were neutralized.[^cite_note-460] On 20 August, separatist commander Tantoh Leonard (known as "General Colabo") was killed by his own fighters in Ndop, following a dispute over the distribution of ransom money.[^cite_note-461] On 21 August, in a video, the separatist group Mountain Lions declared a two-week lockdown starting on 9 September in order to disrupt school resumption.[^cite_note-462] On 23 August, suspected separatists burned a taxi in Bamenda.[^cite_note-463] On 24 August, at least three police officers were killed in Buea. The Mountain Lions claimed responsibility for the attack.[^cite_note-464] On 25 August, a driver for a health NGO was kidnapped and killed by gunmen in Bamenda.[^cite_note-465] On 27 August, a soldier was killed in an alleged separatist attack near Bamenda.[^cite_note-466]

On 2 September, separatist fighters killed a biker and burnt down a taxi in Buea.[^cite_note-467] In Mbatu, near Bamenda, three people were killed by armed separatists.[^cite_note-468] On 8 September, a former separatist fighter was killed in an alleged targeted attack in Bamenda.[^cite_note-469] In Sop, Bui, three separatist fighters were killed in a confrontation with the Cameroonian army.[^cite_note-470] On 16 September, two separatist fighters were killed during an operation by the Rapid Intervention Brigade (BIR) in Worsing, Bali Nyonga. In a separate incident, another separatist fighter was killed in a BIR attack between Sabga, Kedjon Ketinguh and Bamessing.[^cite_note-471] A civilian was also killed in the clashes.[^cite_note-472] On 19 September, a suspected separatist IED detonated and numerous gunshots erupted in Bamenda.[^cite_note-473] On 24 September, AGovC leader Ayaba Cho Lucas was arrested in [Norway](21241.md) and charged with inciting crimes against humanity.[^cite_note-474] AGovC vice president Julius Nyih took over as interim leader of the organization, in accordance with a contingency succession plan.[^cite_note-mimimefosep272024-475]

On 1 October, separatists across the Anglophone regions celebrated Ambazonia's independence day with parades and meetings. In many of the major cities, the streets were deserted due to separatist-imposed ghost towns.[^cite_note-476] Separatists held large parades in rural areas. A group known as the Ambazonia State Army, led by "General Talk and Do", held a military parade in Kikaikom. ADF commander "General Sumanguru" organized a similar parade in Momo department.[^cite_note-talkanddosumanguru-477] On 8 October, Cameroonian forces raided a separatist stronghold in the village of Effa in Momo. A separatist commander known as "General Popo" was killed.[^cite_note-478] On 9 October, two Cameroonian soldiers were killed in a separatist ambush in Mbingo, Boyo department. A separatist group known as the "Pythons of Boyo," led by "General Nyih", claimed responsibility.[^cite_note-479] On 12 October, seven separatist fighters from Widikum surrendered and joined the Disarmament, Demobilization and Reintegration Centre in Bamenda.[^cite_note-480] On 16 October, separatists executed two alleged gang members in Bali Nyonga, after a mob had seized them and handed them over to the separatists.[^cite_note-481] On 18 October, six suspected Ambazonian separatists were arrested by Nigerian police in [Akamkpa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akamkpa), Cross River State.[^cite_note-482] On 26 October, following a public screening of a documentary on President Paul Biya organized by the ruling party, two residents were shot dead and a municipal official was abducted and murdered by unidentified gunmen in Bamenda II.[^cite_note-483][^cite_note-484] Gunmen also attacked the Government Bilingual High School in Bamenda; one student was injured in the attack. No separatist group claimed responsibility.[^cite_note-485] On 29 October, three suspected separatists were arrested by Cameroonian forces in a military operation in Kedjom Ketinguh, Ngo-Ketunjia.[^cite_note-486]

On 2 November, the BIR opened two new bases in Nguti and Alou, with the aim of combating ghost towns and increasing security along the Kumba-Mamfe road.[^cite_note-487] On 4 November, a young man was killed in Mulang, Bamenda. Locals claimed that he was killed by soldiers.[^cite_note-488] On 11 November, Cameroonian soldiers tortured a group of civilians in Ngoketunjia for several hours, killed one of them, and looted his possessions.[^cite_note-489] On 14 November, separatist fighters abducted Langsi Abel, a former mayor of Bafut.[^cite_note-490] He was released after eight days.[^cite_note-491] On 15 November, armed men burned a man to death on Ayaba Street in Bamenda using tyres.[^cite_note-492] On 17 November, separatist fighters attacked a vehicle on the Ndu-Foumban road, killing two civilians and a soldier. One more civilian was injured.[^cite_note-493] The Ambazonia State Army claimed responsibility.[^cite_note-494] On 24 November, Cameroonian forces ambushed and killed at least five ADF fighters in Nchulam, Bamenda, including a commander known as "General Weapon" and his deputy, "Colonel Mami".[^cite_note-495][^cite_note-496] On 29 November, a police officer was killed and another was injured by separatist fighters in Bamenda.[^cite_note-497]

On 6–7 December, an explosion and gunshots were heard in Bamenda, as separatists moved to enforce a lockdown.[^cite_note-498] At least one person was killed.[^cite_note-499] On 11 December, Fon Richard Muntong III of Bamessing issued an ultimatum where he demanded that all separatist fighters leave his village by 31 December.[^cite_note-500] On 13 December, two police officers were seriously injured in a clash with separatist fighters in Bamenda. The ADF claimed responsibility for the attack.[^cite_note-\:3-501] On 14 December, separatists imposed a lockdown in Bamenda to mourn one of their members killed a few weeks earlier by security forces.[^cite_note-\:3-501] On 18 December, the Nigerian Army arrested four suspected Ambazonian separatists in [Takum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takum), Taraba State, Nigeria. The four confessed to being involved with weapons smuggling.[^cite_note-502] On 23 December, Cameroonian soldiers arrested three people in Bamenda. According to local sources, one of them was killed while attempting to escape.[^cite_note-503] On 27 December, separatist fighters reportedly stabbed to death a man known as "Cho Pinyin" in Menka, Pinyin.[^cite_note-504] On 29 December, a magistrate was kidnapped by unidentified gunmen from his home in Bamenda. Many other civilians were abducted the same day by suspected separatists in the town.[^cite_note-505] On 30 December, a separatist fighter known as "Mission Commander" was reportedly killed by military forces in Rom, [Nwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nwa,_Cameroon). In Makobe, Southwest Region, soldiers of the 21st Battalion killed a separatist fighter known as "Colonel Toless".[^cite_note-506]

### 2025

Main article: [Timeline of the Anglophone Crisis (2025)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Anglophone_Crisis_\(2025\))

By March 2025, *[Africa Confidential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Africa_Confidential)* assessed that the Anglophone Crisis had remained a "bloody stalemate", with neither the government nor the rebels in a position to win the conflict.[^cite_note-507] Meanwhile, infighting among the Ambazonia separatists had significantly increased, as had rebel attacks on civilians.[^cite_note-508]

### 2026

Main article: [Timeline of the Anglophone Crisis (2026)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Anglophone_Crisis_\(2026\))

## Strategy

### Military strategy



Approximate military situation, as of October 2023.



Cameroonian soldiers during a skirmish against Ambazonian fighters.

The Cameroonian Army is fighting a [counter-insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-insurgency) war, aiming to hit the separatists' support base. This includes burning houses where weapons are found and, according to locals but denied by the army, carrying out revenge attacks.[^cite_note-Dirty_War-509] In August 2018, the Defence Minister of Cameroon announced that the army would be expanded with 2,600 new recruits, 2,000 of whom would go to the BIR.[^cite_note-510] In addition to expanding the army, the government has supported local vigilante groups, of which there were more than thirty as of October 2019.[^cite_note-xinhuavigilantes-3] The government has also set up rehab centers in Bamenda and Buea to reintegrate separatists who have surrendered into civil society.[^cite_note-511] With no military victory in sight, the Cameroonian Army has sought to at least contain the conflict to rural areas. As of late-2018, the Cameroonian Army aimed to control all urban areas as well as strategic points on the countryside, and did not seek to recapture the entire Anglophone regions.[^cite_note-crisisgroupmay22019-6] In some cases, this has involved a mutual tacit understanding on who controls certain areas; separatist and military checkpoints are sometimes in close proximity of each other, without either side attacking.[^cite_note-fodorssep232020-512] Cameroon's strategy of prioritizing urban areas began to yield results during the first half of 2020; by June, the separatists had been largely pushed out of Buea.[^cite_note-theafricanreportjun252020-255]



Weapons of separatist fighters in Bamenda, seized by the Cameroonian military in February 2019

The Ambazonian separatists are fighting a [guerrilla war](12720.md). Numerically and materially disadvantaged, the separatists carry out [hit-and-run attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hit-and-run_tactics), [ambushes](1905.md) and [raids](6873191.md). According to the ADF, as of June 2018 there were 1,500 soldiers in the ADF, spread across 20 camps throughout Southern Cameroons.[^cite_note-EmmanuelFreudenthal-513] In May 2019, independent sources measured the total number of separatist fighters to be between 2,000 and 4,000, a figure that included numerous ex-soldiers and ex-policemen, a significant number of female combatants, and dozens of Nigerian mercenaries. The Nigerians among the rebels mostly consisted of criminals and ex-insurgents who had previously taken part in the [conflict in the Niger Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conflict_in_the_Niger_Delta).[^cite_note-crisisgroupmay22019-6] Many separatist militias, including the ADF and SOCADEF, are directly commanded by diaspora leaders in the United States, Norway, Germany and other countries; aid agencies have oftentimes had to negotiate with these leaders over the phone, rather than with separatists on the ground.[^cite_note-Economist09092019-514]

At the start of the war, the separatists struggled with gaining access to firearms, having to rely on [hunting rifles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rifle) and in some cases single-shot pistols. As the war went on, they gradually gained limited access to some more sophisticated weapons, capturing some from the Cameroonian Army and buying some in Nigeria (where they enjoyed the support of officers in the Nigerian Army). They also benefitted from fundraising campaigns (including the introduction of their own [cryptocurrency](36662188.md), the [AmbaCoin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AmbaCoin)), launched by diaspora activists to purchase guns for the separatist militias. This began to yield visible results in the first half of 2019.[^cite_note-goinginternational-515] From being severely outgunned in the fall of 2018,[^cite_note-viceoct2018-111] by the summer of 2019 the separatists had become well-armed.[^cite_note-voajun192019-169] They also declared that they had started producing their own weapons within Southern Cameroons, a declaration followed shortly by an explosion in Mamfe that killed four policemen.[^cite_note-producingownweapons-168] By November 2020, the separatists' manufacturing of weapons within the Anglophone regions had become a serious problem for Cameroon.[^cite_note-516] IEDs in particular proved effective for the separatists, and their frequent use, either alone or as part of ambushes, started to cause heavy Cameroonian casualties from the beginning of 2021.[^cite_note-aljazeeraapr12021-10] Cameroon only has a limited number of mine-protected armored vehicles, and IED attacks have been getting increasingly sophisticated (including remote detonation). This has caused severe loss of morale among Cameroonian soldiers.[^cite_note-africanargumentsaug242021-517] In addition, some Ambazonian militias began partnering with [Biafran](44431.md) separatists, who helped them access the [arms black market](42210.md) in Nigeria.[^cite_note-dwjul312021-518] In September 2021, following an ambush in which 15 soldiers were killed and two armored vehicles destroyed, Cameroon's Defense Minister Joseph Beti Assomo announced a "paradigm shift" in the war.[^cite_note-519]

The militias enjoy significant local support, with civilians giving them food, informing them on troop movements, or outright assisting them in carrying out attacks.[^cite_note-BBCoct42018-72] In separatist-controlled territory, a common [social contract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_contract) is that civilians develop close connections with separatist fighters, who in turn defend them against Cameroonian raids.[^cite_note-fodorssep232020-512] Unlike most Cameroonian soldiers deployed in the region, the separatists are locals, and are thus more familiar with the terrain. Cameroonian General Melingui stated that the separatists have a leverage over the army when it comes to familiarity with the battleground; "They know the terrain. These are youths from local villages. We try to seek them out but we can't find them. Our men aren't familiar with the forest." Cameroonian authorities have admitted that they have little control over the security situation outside the cities.[^cite_note-Dirty_War-509] Journalist Emmanuel Freudenthal, who spent a week with ADF rebels in 2018, stated that the separatists controlled much of the countryside because the infrastructure in Southern Cameroons is so poorly developed, making it hard for the Cameroonian government to access those areas.[^cite_note-EmmanuelFreudenthal-513]

The IG has stressed that the war will take place solely within Southern Cameroons, and claims that attacks across the border have been [false flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_flag) operations by the Cameroonian government.[^cite_note-bareta25dec2018-155] This stance is not shared by AGovC. In March 2019, the ADF announced that it would take the war into the French-speaking parts of Cameroon, defying the IG.[^cite_note-jdc2apr2019-154] The ADF allied itself with the [Indigenous People of Biafra](49020788.md) and its armed wing, the [Eastern Security Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Security_Network) in 2021, and declared that this alliance would entail joint military operations.[^cite_note-foreignpolicymay202021-306] AGovC leader [Ayaba Cho Lucas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayaba_Cho_Lucas) summed up the ADF strategy in the following way: "60% of the GDP of Cameroon is earned in Ambazonia. \[...] We must try to raise the cost of the occupation to higher than the profits they get here."[^cite_note-thisisagenocide-93]

Retired American diplomat [Herman Jay Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herman_Jay_Cohen) has argued that Cameroon's military approach to the conflict plays straight into the strategy of the separatists. Drawing parallels to the [Eritrean War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eritrean_War_of_Independence), he has claimed that the growing bitterness resulting from prolonged warfare will only serve to close the window of opportunity where reconciliation and territorial integrity is still an option, thereby – ironically – increasing the likelihood of Ambazonian secession.[^cite_note-520]

### Political, diplomatic and propaganda strategy

According to Millan Atam, chairperson of the Southern Cameroonians Congress of the People, the separatists are building up support for their cause through two distinct phases. The first phase was to build internal capability to resist the Cameroonian Army and raise faith in the cause. Once a significant portion of the population of Southern Cameroons clearly wanted separation, the separatists would approach the international community with their cause.[^cite_note-521]

The Cameroonian government has tried to limit the extent of which the conflict affects everyday life in Southern Cameroons, and portrays the war as a battle between chaos and stability in which the government represents the latter. To this end, local authorities have penalized businesses that respected "ghost towns" declared by the separatists.[^cite_note-522] The government has fired and replaced local administrators who fled from the region, despite their fears of kidnappings.[^cite_note-523] In order to fight the separatist school boycott, according to local sources in Ndop, Cameroonian soldiers have forced children to attend school, often through violence and threats.[^cite_note-524]

To prevent the Anglophone Crisis from becoming a full-fledged intercommunal war, Cameroonian authorities have sought to gain as much support among the Anglophone population as possible. In August 2018, [Minister of Territorial Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Territorial_Administration_\(Cameroon\)) Atanga Nji offered amnesty to separatists who surrender their weapons, saying they would "be received as prodigal sons". The minister also announced a plan to rebuild infrastructure that had been destroyed due to the conflict.[^cite_note-525] In February 2021, the government claimed that 4,000 Anglophones had applied to join the military, including hundreds of former separatist fighters.[^cite_note-526]

Both sides have used [WhatsApp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WhatsApp) to spread propaganda.[^cite_note-Dirty_War-509] Cameroonian authorities have arrested journalists on the accusation of propagating false information, the punishment for which is six months to two years in prison.[^cite_note-527]

## War crimes

### By Cameroonian forces

There is photographic evidence that shows a consistent strategy of burning down villages by Cameroonian forces. The army has claimed that the soldiers who were filmed were separatists wearing stolen Cameroonian Army uniforms, a claim that has been denied by local residents. Satellite images show extensive damage to villages. Journalists have been denied entry to the conflict zones, and soldiers have been forbidden from carrying mobile phones.[^cite_note-burning-528] In August 2018, the [Centre for Human Rights and Democracy in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_for_Human_Rights_and_Democracy_in_Africa) published a list of 105 villages that had been raided by government forces since October 2017. Citing eyewitness accounts, videos and photos as evidence, the Centre claimed that 71 of these villages had been completely destroyed and depopulated, while the remaining 34 had been partially deserted.[^cite_note-529]

With IEDs becoming a growing threat to its soldiers, the BIR has allegedly forced civilians to act as human [minesweepers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minesweeping).[^cite_note-africanargumentsaug242021-517]

### By separatist forces



A destroyed bilingual high school in [Fontem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fontem).

At the end of 2017, the separatists declared a school boycott, and have attacked and burnt down schools that refused to shut down. Between February 2017 and May 2018, at least 42 schools were targeted.[^cite_note-burning-528] Some separatists consider schools to be legitimate targets because the French language is taught as a mandatory subject.[^cite_note-EmmanuelFreudenthal-513] As of July 2019, close to 6,000 schools had shut down across the Anglophone regions, affecting more than 600,000 children.[^cite_note-530] Separatists began opening their own schools in September 2019, but these were insufficient to cover the educational needs in the areas they controlled.[^cite_note-community_schools-197] In September 2020, prominent separatists Mark Bareta and Eric Tataw called for an end to the school boycott.[^cite_note-531] However, this was rejected by Julius Ayuk Tabe, Samuel Ikome Sako and Ayaba Cho Lucas, all of whom insisted that a ceasefire had to precede school resumption.[^cite_note-532] Attacks on schools have cost the separatists some loss of support among locals who had used to sympathize with their cause.[^cite_note-dwjan42021-533] In September 2021, the Ambazonia Defence Forces gave instructions for schools to reopen.[^cite_note-534]

In their effort to make the Anglophone regions ungovernable, separatist elements have maimed employees at state-owned corporations.[^cite_note-Cameroon_2019-535] Kidnapping for ransom has also occurred frequently,[^cite_note-536] as well as blackmailing of civilians into transferring money to fund the struggle.[^cite_note-537] Owing to the obscure nature of many of the [separatist militias](60554443.md), some attacks attributed to separatists may as well be the acts of local criminals. Notwithstanding, it is beyond doubt that separatist elements have carried out numerous such acts,[^cite_note-538] sometimes drawing condemnation from the Interim Government of Ambazonia.[^cite_note-Cameroon_2019-535]

Throughout 2017, there were no reports of ADF using violence against civilians. As of October 2018, five such incidents had been reported, though these attacks were for the most part not lethal; one civilian death was attributed to an ADF attack. Other separatist groups had attacked civilians 25 times within the same time frame, and were responsible for 13 civilian deaths.[^cite_note-acleddatapickingafight-14] Separatists have also been accused of using schools and churches as military barracks;[^cite_note-539] in July 2019, Cameroon accused the separatists of occupying more than 50 schools.[^cite_note-540] The Ambazonian forces have also engaged in widespread extortion of the civilian population in the Anglophone regions.[^cite_note-thenewhumanitarian.org-20]

In 2021, some separatist fighters began dressing up in captured Cameroonian military uniforms as a [ruse of war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruse_of_war),[^cite_note-541] violating the [Hague Convention of 1907](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hague_Conventions_of_1899_and_1907#Hague_Convention_of_1907) *IV*, Article 23.[^cite_note-542]

## Casualties

Between September 2017 and February 2018, Cameroon claimed to have lost 22 soldiers and policemen in the conflict.[^cite_note-543] By May, at least 44 soldiers and policemen had been killed.[^cite_note-unfoldingcatastrophe-8] In June 2018, the official figure rose to 84 dead.[^cite_note-544] Within two weeks in the later half of June, the official figure went up to more than 120 dead.[^cite_note-100deadvoa-95] By October 2018, the military and police had lost at least 175 servicemen. Separatist losses were estimated to be in the hundreds.[^cite_note-reliefweboct10-545]

The war intensified in early 2019. By June, Cameroonian military and police casualties were estimated to be around 500 dead. The separatists had lost around 1,000 fighters.[^cite_note-unfoldingcatastrophe-8] While civilian casualties are hard to determine, in May 2019 they were estimated by independent sources to be around 650 killed, out of a total death toll of 1,850.[^cite_note-crisisgroupmay22019-6] In January the same year, the [Diocese of Kumbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Kumbo) had been able to document 385 civilian deaths in Kumbo alone within the past seven months.[^cite_note-546] In September 2019, independent sources stated that the conflict had claimed the lives of around 3,000 people, counting both combatants and civilians.[^cite_note-3000killed-547] By February 2020, between 800 and 1,000 Cameroonian soldiers had been killed.[^cite_note-newhumanitarianfeb62020-7] In addition, at least 250 ethnic [Mbororos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wodaabe) had been killed as of July 2020, counting both civilians and pro-Cameroonian militants.[^cite_note-548] Cameroonian casualties spiked in 2021, as separatists were becoming increasingly well-armed.[^cite_note-dwjul312021-518]

Anglophone groups have disputed these figures. While federalist movements claimed that the conflict had taken 3,000–5,000 lives as of the summer of 2019, separatists claimed that between 5,000 and 10,000 people had been killed.[^cite_note-unfoldingcatastrophe-8]

## Humanitarian consequences



[Internally displaced people](427408.md) from the Anglophone regions, in [Douala](19997263.md), Cameroon, March 2020.

By January 2018, 15,000 people had fled from Southern Cameroons to Nigeria.[^cite_note-Africa-12] This number increased to at least 40,000 people by February.[^cite_note-549] By August 2018, more than 180,000 people had been displaced due to the war.[^cite_note-atrisk230818-550] As of May 2019, 530,000 people had been internally displaced, and 35,000 had fled to Nigeria.[^cite_note-crisisgroupmay22019-6] In June 2019, [UNICEF](19698110.md) said that 1.3 million people in the Anglophone regions needed humanitarian aid.[^cite_note-551]

It is clear that Anglophones and Francophones have experienced the crisis differently. Anglophones lost their family and friends in some of the riots. Some of them were severely beaten and many of them were arrested during the protests. The government cut the internet on the Anglophone side for about three months. Due to the lack of teachers, burned schools and the unsafe feeling of going to school, many Anglophones lost years of school. With the crisis boiling came the rise of separatists who committed inhumane acts. The separatists in Cameroon are "fighting to create an independent Anglophone state of "Ambazonia" since 2016".[^cite_note-\:02-552] Armed separatist groups are kidnapping, terrorizing, and killing civilians across the English-speaking regions with no apparent fear of being held to account by either their own leaders or Cameroonian law enforcement".[^cite_note-\:02-552]

By 2024, Northwest Region had become the second most dangerous administrative division in Africa for civilians, with only [Gezira State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gezira_State) in [Sudan](27421.md) being more dangerous.[^cite_note-553]

## Other consequences

The conflict has severely harmed the local economy. In June 2018, Cameroon Development Corporation, a state-owned company with 22,000 employees, declared the conflict could lead to the loss of 5,000 jobs on the short term.[^cite_note-554] In July 2018, Cameroonian NGO *Human Is Right* reported that the war had caused a 70 percent increase in unemployment in the agricultural sector. The palm oil and cocoa sectors in Southwest Region had taken a severe blow, with state-owned company Pamol abandoning plantations in some areas. The private company Telcar Cocoa reported that the cocoa production had fallen 80 percent. The NGO suggested that companies make deals with the separatists in order to safeguard their facilities.[^cite_note-555] By 2019, annual tax revenues in the Anglophone regions had dropped from US\$800,000 to US\$1,000, and by 2020 almost all tax officials had fled their crossing posts on the Nigerian border.[^cite_note-556] The separatists aim to prevent the Cameroonian state from getting any income from the Anglophone regions, in order to make cost of controlling the region surpass the benefits.[^cite_note-thisisagenocide-93]

The conflict has triggered an exodus of the Nigerian business community from Southern Cameroons, as well as Nigerian traders who used to run key markets.[^cite_note-557]

Thousands of displaced people have fled to protected natural areas, endangering the wildlife there.[^cite_note-558]

## Reactions



Anglophone Cameroonian expats marching in support of the Ambazonian cause

### Within Cameroon

The Anglophone Crisis has become a divisive question in Cameroonian politics. The [Cameroon People's Democratic Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameroon_People's_Democratic_Movement) (CPDM), the ruling party, regards the separatists as terrorists and supports a military solution to the conflict.[^cite_note-559] The CPDM supported both the carrying out and conclusion of the [Major National Dialogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_National_Dialogue), which was organized by the Cameroonian government.[^cite_note-Cameroon_2019._p_12-190] As of September 2020, the Cameroonian government was divided on the subject of talks with the separatists, with one camp, including the prime minister, advocating a negotiated end of the war, and the other camp opposing negotiations.[^cite_note-wptsep222020-560]

Meanwhile, the opposition has been vocal in its criticism of the government's handling of the conflict. In January 2019, the [Social Democratic Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Front_\(Cameroon\)) announced it would oppose any future elections in the country while the war is still ongoing. The party supports a negotiated solution to the conflict, and has demanded a ceasefire, the opening of a dialogue, amnesty for everyone jailed because of the crisis, the establishment of a [truth and reconciliation commission](3154383.md), and decentralization of the country.[^cite_note-561] In March 2019, the SDF accused members of the Cameroonian government of supporting certain armed elements in the Anglophone regions.[^cite_note-562] In May 2019, the SDF announced it would boycott [National Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Day_\(Cameroon\)) celebrations in sympathy with people in Southern Cameroons living in a state of civil war.[^cite_note-nationalday2019-563]

Smaller opposition parties, such as the [Cameroon Renaissance Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameroon_Renaissance_Movement) (MRC), also blame the government for failing to solve the Anglophone Crisis.[^cite_note-564] On 26 January 2019, supporters of the MRC invaded the Cameroonian embassy in Paris, citing – among other reasons – the Anglophone Crisis.[^cite_note-565] In May 2019, the MRC joined the SDF in boycotting National Day celebrations.[^cite_note-nationalday2019-563] In August 2020, MRC leader [Maurice Kamto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maurice_Kamto) threatened to start a "gigantic national campaign" to overthrow President Biya if the latter convened the electorate without first solving the Anglophone Crisis.[^cite_note-566] The Cameroon People's Party has taken a nuanced approach, condemning both the government and the separatists. Party leader [Kah Walla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kah_Walla) has said that the crisis can be solved by turning Cameroon into a federation.[^cite_note-567]

## Mediation

In 2019, the President of Cameroon initiated a 5-day dialogue between both parties.[^cite_note-\:32-568] However, the dialogue was unsuccessful for different reasons. One of them being that separatists invited to the conversation were either in prison or were scared of being arrested by the government. "Their fears were credible" as most of the separatists leaders that were arrested earlier were "handed life sentences on charges of terrorism".[^cite_note-569]

Later mediation efforts also stalled. in May 2025, former South African president [Thabo Mbeki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thabo_Mbeki) revealed that President Biya had rejected an attempt by a group of former African heads of state to mediate the conflict, and subsequent appeals for dialogue, including a papal call for peace talks, went unheeded.[^cite_note-570]

### Countries and international organizations

Main article: [International reactions to the Anglophone Crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_reactions_to_the_Anglophone_Crisis)

The Anglophone Crisis has become a diplomatic challenge to Cameroon, and has damaged the country's relations with some of its allies. While member states of the [African Union](10634933.md)[^cite_note-AU-571] and [France](5843419.md)[^cite_note-Arria-572] have taken either a neutral stance or sided with the Cameroonian government, several European countries and the [United States](3434750.md) have criticized Cameroon.[^cite_note-FalloutsUN-573] The United States has been particularly vocal in its criticism; In July 2019, following a fact-finding mission by some of its members to the country, the [House of Representatives](19468510.md) called for a reintroduction of a federal system in Cameroon.[^cite_note-574]

In June 2019, Switzerland announced that both the Cameroonian government and the separatists had asked it to act as a mediator, and that negotiations would take place. This was the first known case of talks between the two warring sides, and was met with international endorsement.[^cite_note-575] However, the talks ultimately failed.[^cite_note-reutersjul42020-18] The talks were referred to as the "Swiss Process" but were only supported by one faction of the independence movement. [Sisiku Julius Ayuk Tabe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sisiku_Julius_Ayuk_Tabe) and [Ayaba Cho Lucas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayaba_Cho_Lucas) both opposed them, and the Cameroonian Government did not buy into the talks either, rendering them ineffective. The faction of the movement aligned with [Samuel Ikome Sako](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Ikome_Sako) has continued to insist that the talks facilitated by Switzerland are the only means of resolving the conflict.[^cite_note-thenewhumanitarian.org-20]
